A NICE LONG EVENING
I
THE snow had held off that year until February. But
on Washington's Birthday the great flakes came down
all day; and indoors the light had that peculiar white-
ness which will tell you, even if you have your back
to the windows, that it is snowing.
Mrs. Meigs always loved to wake to a snow-storm.
It took her back to the days when, if it was snowing
in the morning, she used to keep her children home
from school, and have an open fire of cannel coal, and
establish herself snugly for the day, with her chicks
huddled about her. She had enjoyed those rare days all
the more because she wasn't in general a hovering sort
of mother. She enjoyed the recollection all the more
because she made a point of not sitting down too often
to recall the past.
When you've turned eighty, it doesn't do to think
too much about the past. Anyhow she never had much
time for reminiscence. The present was always too ac-
tively interesting; this winter especially, it seemed to
her that she had had hardly a minute to herself.
At lunch time, indeed, she was still hoping that she
could get out this afternoon. It happened to be Satur-
day, and on Saturday she always seemed to have errands
to be done. Her books must go back to the circulating
library; it would be horrid to have Sunday catch you
34
A NICE LONG EVENING
with nothing new in the house to read. She wanted to
take a pair of shoes to the cobbler's to have the heels
straightened; she wanted some roast chestnuts from
the stand two blocks down, at the street car intersec-
tion.
Of course she could always have her errands done
for her; but she liked talking to the cobbler, and the
roast chestnut man, and the clerk who attended to the
circulating library in the drug-store. The children
would all have their sleds out this afternoon, too; and
every child for blocks was her friend. She didn't even
know the names of some of them; but they all knew
hers. Newcomers to the block were promptly initiated:
"Don't you know who that is? That's Mrs. Meigs!"
A short walk was likely to last two hours, if she took
it in her own neighborhood. It was no wonder she had
a lot of work for the cobbler.
After lunch, however, she gave up the idea of get-
ting out this afternoon. The wind had risen now,
and the snow was drifting. Mrs. Meigs telephoned to
Mrs. Bishop, who lived just across the hall in the same
building, and wouldn't have to go outdoors in order
to come and spend the afternoon with her.
Mrs. Bishop brought her patchwork; and Mrs. Meigs,
after some hunting, found her knitting. "Unless you'd
rather play a game?" she hinted. "You generally beat
when we play double Canfield."
"I played that with my son-in-law the other night,"
said Mrs. Bishop indignantly. "Do you know, after the
first game I discovered that he was letting me win? You
might think I was a six-year-old child!"
"I might think he was," said Mrs. Meigs. "I could
let you win, and you'd never be any the wiser."
"I suppose Grace told him to let me win," went on
Mrs. Bishop, after she had smiled at the joke. "GraceA NICE LONG EVENING
5
is a devoted daughter, and I'm sure I don't know what
I'd do without her. But she always wants to do my
thinking for me."
"That's the worst of having an only daughter," said
Mrs. Meigs. "My tribe are devoted too; but there are
so many of them, I generally manage to slip through
the gaps. This afternoon for instance, before you came
in, Edith and Natalie both telephoned. But neither of
them had me on her mind to the extent of feeling
herself responsible for my day. Jack's wife called up,
too; she wanted a recipe for muffins. That call I was
glad to answer. Jack's been married for fifteen years,
but he's been keeping house only a few months. His
wife seems to have the right idea, though, if she was
a little late in getting it."
"Didn't your oldest daughter call up?" asked
Mrs. Bishop. "That is, maybe I oughtn't to have asked.
But you used to see so much of her-"
Mrs. Meigs chuckled. "Millicent considers me a total
loss, I'm afraid, since she had all the arrangements made
for moving me into her house, and then at the last
minute I didn't go."
"I was just sick at the idea that you were going,"
confided Mrs. Bishop. "That is, of course it's natural
for a daughter to want her mother to live with her.
But I'm sure it's so nice and cozy here-"
"I'm sure I'm not going to move out of this flat,"
said Mrs. Meigs, "until they take me out feet foremost."
Mrs. Bishop repressed a shudder at her friend's lan-
guage. In her girlhood she had learned to say that people
"passed on"; and if she alluded to Mr. Bishop's death,
though it was now twenty years in the past, her phrase
was always, "When my great sorrow came." Although
she admired Mrs. Meigs tremendously, Mrs. Bishop was
often embarrassed by her friend's directness.6
A NICE LONG EVENING
"You've had company in the flat this winter, of
course," she hastened to say. "That Mrs. O'Neil-of
course she's a sort of relative-"
"Can you tell me what sort? Her late husband was
a cousin of the young fellow that married my grand-
daughter. That makes Molly what to me?" demanded
Mrs. Meigs.
Mrs. Bishop did not try to guess the riddle; but she
lowered her voice suitably to ask, "Do you think
Mrs. O'Neil is getting over the shock of her husband's
death?"
Mrs. Meigs frowned a little. "I can't tell, I'm sure.
Molly is a closed book to me. I took her in to get her
out of Tip's way. I'm afraid, though, that I expected her
to be something like Tip. There was no sense in that, of
course. His cousin's widow is no kin to Tip."
"They're both Irish," suggested Mrs. Bishop.
"Molly's the other kind of Irish," said Mrs. Meigs.
"Tip talks all the time, and Molly never has a word to
say for herself. Of course, maybe that's all the better
while I have her right here in the house. She never gets
in anybody's way, anyhow."
Mrs. Bishop would have liked to go on asking questions.
Molly O'Neil had been wife, mother and widow all in
a single year, and all while she was still under twenty.
Mrs. Bishop considered her a legitimate object of inter-
est: you could hardly call it gossip, if you alluded to her
always as "poor Molly." But Mrs. Meigs was already set-
ting up the card table; and she went as far as the kitchen
to tell Anna to make them some coffee.
Three o'clock was an odd hour to be drinking coffee;
but when it came, Mrs. Bishop did not refuse it. The
room was cozy; the janitor gave them plenty of heat in
this old building. The wind was now blowing the snow
against the windows, where it made the faintest hissingA NICE LONG EVENING
7
sound. And Mrs. Meigs was beyond suspicion of letting
Mrs. Bishop win at double Canfield; in fact she won the
first two games herself.
They were laying out the cards for a third game when
the door of the living-room opened. On the threshold
stood a dark-haired girl in a shabby black dress. In her
arms she carried a baby about six months old: a blue-
eyed baby, all waving legs and arms, with only the faint-
est down to show where his hair would some day grow.
"It's snowing so hard, I thought I wouldn't take him
out in his buggy to-day," said the girl. "But the doctor
told me I should get a walk every day myself."
"You can leave the baby here, and I'll watch him,"
said Mrs. Meigs. "He's had his afternoon nap, hasn't he?"
"He won't sleep again till he's put to bed for the
night." Molly began to arrange pillows on the daven-
port; she propped the baby among them, flat on his
back.
"He's growing fast, isn't he?" asked Mrs. Bishop.
"He makes his gain every week," said Molly.
Mrs. Bishop watched her walk back to the dining-
room. She noted the sleek curves of Molly's hair, black
as a crow's wing, the soft white of her young neck
against her hair, the little droop of her shoulders, as if
she still felt the weight of the baby after she had laid
him down. "She still wears black for her husband," she
said softly.
"She hasn't got around to change her dress at three in
the afternoon, if that's what you mean," said Mrs.
Meigs. Her tone was not harsh, but it was indifferent. It
would have been all the same to her if Molly had blacked
her face, or sported a wig, or shaved her eyebrows. To
her, Molly was simply the person who brought the
baby in.
Mrs. Bishop joined her at the davenport. The baby8
A NICE LONG EVENING
looked from one to the other of the old ladies; he smiled
at them both. Then he caught Mrs. Bishop's eye, and
gurgled.
Mrs. Bishop was flattered. "Why," she breathed, "I
think he knows me!"
Mrs. Meigs chuckled, but did not contradict her.
"Wouldn't you like to pick him up?" she suggested.
"Oh, I'd love it!" said Mrs. Bishop. "But nowadays
babies aren't supposed to be picked up, are they?"
"They're always having a nap or a bath or a feeding
or something," said Mrs. Meigs. "It isn't often a person
gets a chance at them."
This was a rare chance. A sense of guilt mingled with
Mrs. Bishop's pleasure; and just as she got both hands
behind his back the baby kicked and wriggled, and for
one dreadful moment she thought she was going to drop
him. But although it was years since she had picked up a
baby, she hadn't forgotten how. When she was safely
seated with him in her lap, her wrinkled old face flushed
with delight. She leaned down and kissed him on the
warm top of his head, where the down wasn't thick
enough even to show what color his hair was going to be.
Mrs. Meigs took a rocking-chair opposite, and beamed
at them. Mrs. Bishop was still in her sixties, but she
looked more than her age. When she held the pink-
cheeked baby on her lap, the two of them represented
the two ends of life. It was a charming picture, and
Mrs. Meigs enjoyed it.
But she didn't enjoy it enough to let it alone. "He's
learning to give his hands," she said. She leaned closer.
"Do you want to come, Sonny? Give-your-hands-if
-you-want-to-come."
Sonny had not got the trick perfect. He started to
put out his hands, and then in his excitement drew them
back before Mrs. Meigs could seize them. Losing pa-A NICE LONG EVENING
9
tience, he hurled himself bodily at Mrs. Meigs. She
snatched him and settled him on her knee. He crowed,
and she chuckled.
"I suppose he'd come to you, away from anybody,"
said Mrs. Bishop rather forlornly. To her own ears, that
sounded too much like a hint. She hastened to ask, "You
call him Sonny now?"
"He was named for his father, but it doesn't seem
natural to call a baby by a dead person's name," ex-
plained Mrs. Meigs. "Anyhow, Dennis isn't a name I
care much about. It sounds too much like these Pat-and-
Mike stories. But we couldn't go on forever calling him
just 'the baby. "
"He's a lot of company for you," said Mrs. Bishop.
"With your own grandchildren grown, it certainly is
nice for you to have a baby around the place again."
"He's the only baby in the building," said Mrs. Meigs
proudly. "The other day Molly left his buggy in the
downstairs hall, and I got there just as Mr. Perry from
the first floor was falling over it. Do you know him, by
the way?"
"I've seen him in the hall, I think," said Mrs. Bishop.
"An oldish man, isn't he?"
"Retired clergyman," said Mrs. Meigs. "He and his
wife have lived here about a year, right under me. I said
politely that I hoped the baby didn't disturb them-
meaning of course when he cries, as any baby does some-
times. 'Disturb?' says he politely. 'No child ever disturbs
us. We love to hear the patter of his little feet. "
"The patter of his little-?" Mrs. Bishop was puzzled.
"A six-months-old child!" said Mrs. Meigs scornfully.
"Sonny doesn't even crawl yet."
"What do you suppose Mr. Perry heard?" asked
Mrs. Bishop.
"He didn't know, any more than you do," said10
A NICE LONG EVENING
Mrs. Meigs patiently. "He was just trying to be po-
lite."
Mrs. Bishop leaned over and kissed one of Sonny's wav-
ing fists. "I never had any grandchildren," she said wist-
fully.
"Oh, my dear!" said Mrs. Meigs. "When a woman
gets to our age, all the babies in the world are our grand-
children, really."
Mrs. Bishop beamed. She didn't even stop to resent
the phrase "our age," as she had technical grounds for
doing; Mrs. Meigs, though the younger of the two for
all practical purposes, was fourteen years older than
Mrs. Bishop by the calendar. Mrs. Bishop was so pleased
by the idea of being Sonny's grandmother that
Mrs. Meigs postponed the demands of her conscience,
which had been urging that she give the baby back to
her.
She had to give him back anyhow a moment later: the
phone rang. "That's the 'steenth call to-day," said
Mrs. Meigs, rising to answer.
"You get more phone calls than anybody else I
know," said Mrs. Bishop admiringly.
Mrs. Meigs's answers over the telephone were terse.
When she hung up, she reported, "A snow-storm is a
great institution. That was Dr. Manning. He said he'd
been hoping to get over to see me this evening, but he
was afraid the snow would make that impossible. I was
too much of a lady to say 'Hurrah!' I told him to call
me up next week."
"I think you're hard on Dr. Manning," said
Mrs. Bishop. "He's always so nice and cheerful."
"If you can call
Mrs. Meigs scornfully.

Feeling Sonny warm and soft in the curve of her
arm, Mrs. Bishop was bolder than usual in argument.A NICE LONG EVENING
II
"Personally," she insisted, "I like to talk to an older
man."
"Well," said Mrs. Meigs, "I don't. I was awfully fond
of Cal Fletcher. I still can't get used to the idea that
he's dead. But a cranky old man like Dr. Manning, with
his hair always straggling over his collar, and that big
umbrella hung over his arm, rain or shine-!”
"Dr. Manning admires you," said Mrs. Bishop firmly.
"He admires you very much."
"I know," groaned Mrs. Meigs. "If he'd stick to talk-
ing about his botany, I wouldn't mind him so much,
even if he does make my ears ache. But he always thinks
he has to be gallant!"
"I still think-" argued Mrs. Bishop.
"Oh, all right, all right! I guess if you come right
down to it," Mrs. Meigs confessed, "I don't care so
much about old men. Old ladies are a whole lot nicer, as
a matter of fact."
"Well, just between the two of us, and seeing there
are no men around" began Mrs. Bishop.
She never got beyond that introduction. Mrs. Meigs
suddenly cocked her head, snatched the baby away from
Mrs. Bishop, and landed him back among the sofa pil-
lows. An instant later the door into the dining-room
opened. Molly came in, with her hat and coat on.
Mrs. Bishop squirmed. They had so nearly been
caught holding the baby; and of course nowadays that
was against all the rules. But Mrs. Meigs looked around
innocently. "Better not try to go too far to-day, Molly,"
she advised. "The drifts are getting pretty deep."
"I'll just walk around our own block a few times,
maybe," said Molly. "Then if I get tired, I'll be close to
home. But I'll be passing the drug-store where you get
your books, Mrs. Meigs. Do you want me to get some-
thing for you to read over Sunday?"12
A NICE LONG EVENING
"Yes, I wish you would," said Mrs. Meigs. "Get at
least two novels. Don't let the clerk help you pick them
out, though. You wouldn't believe there are so many
pious novels as he tries to wish onto me."
Molly pulled up the baby's knitted boots, pulled down
his dress, and went out without another word. It was
nice of her to think about those library books; it would
make a great difference to Mrs. Meigs's evening.
It had been a beautiful day all around. But along late
in any day, Mrs. Meigs always began to look forward to
the evening. These short winter days always meant a
nice long evening. You could turn on your best lamp
and establish yourself with your book; and the longer
the evening lasted, the better.
This was Saturday, too: which meant that breakfast
would be late to-morrow, and Mrs. Meigs could pay less
attention than ever to the distant threat of bedtime.
Sunday would be a busy day: Jack's wife was having
the family connection to supper in her new bungalow,
and Jack had told his mother that he would drive over
for her about the middle of the afternoon.
She had a well arranged life. Her four children were
all married and settled; her granddaughter married
too, and her grandson in college. And as the grandson
put it, "None of us have ever gone to jail." Nice people
her family were, especially since she had succeeded in
keeping them in their proper place. She would have
hated to be like Mrs. Bishop, living with a married
daughter who smothered her with "devotion." Devotion
was like steam heat. You wanted it, of course, but you
didn't want too much of it.
From the safe port of her eighty years, she sometimes
pitied younger people. Sometimes she envied them. The
happiest days of her life had been when her children
were little, and she and her husband had encountered allA NICE LONG EVENING
13
the crises of bringing up a young family. But she hadn't
realized then how happy those days were. She got the
full flavor of them now; and the long and lovely past,
though it was seldom in her mind as much as it had
been to-day, still made a background for the moment's
enjoyment.
Dusk was drawing in now, but she wouldn't shut out
the snow-storm. She left the shades undrawn, even
when she turned on a light. But when the lights were
turned on, the long contented evening was in fact be-
ginning.
Mrs. Bishop's eyes followed her from one to another
of the four lamps in her living-room. Mrs. Meigs liked
a lot of light; and unless she was going to settle herself
with a book, she generally had all the lamps going at
once.
"I went to sleep the other night with the lights in my
bedroom still on," confided Mrs. Bishop. "Since then
Grace has been putting them out for me when she went
to bed. I'm not always sleepy so early. But then, I know
she means it for the best."
"Of course she does," said Mrs. Meigs heartily-to her
own ears, just a little too heartily. "Don't you want an-
other cup of coffee? That silver pot keeps it hot."
"Oh, I couldn't drink another drop! I suppose,” said
Mrs. Bishop wistfully, "you wouldn't care for another
game of double Canfield? You won both those we did
play, you know."
"Then I'll have to give you your revenge," said
Mrs. Meigs promptly. But instead of picking up the
cards, she stooped over the baby. And the baby, recog-
nizing her, gurgled and lunged at her.
"I'm sure it's wonderful, the way he always wants to
go to you," said Mrs. Bishop loyally. "I believe he'd go
to you, away from his own mother.".14
A NICE LONG EVENING
"Oh, I wouldn't try that!" said Mrs. Meigs hastily.
"He likes you too, you know."
"Not so well as he does you," insisted Mrs. Bishop.
Mrs. Meigs felt a glow of triumph. Sonny was in a
sense her property, and she liked to have her rights
recognized. She leaned over to pick him up.
Then across her mind's eye came a picture of
Mrs. Bishop tucked away in her lonely bedroom, with
the lights turned out for her, and sleep long in coming.
And at the same hour, and perhaps much later,
Mrs. Meigs would be sitting cozily and independently in
her warm living-room, with her book and her knitting,
and perhaps one of her children or grandchildren ring-
ing up to say good-night.
She straightened up. "I think we'd better be caught
playing cards, not breaking rules," she said. "But if you
wanted to sit down there on the davenport with Sonny,
you could hold him while you played. Then if you got
tired of holding him, you could put him down beside
you."
"If anybody ought to hold him, you ought," pro-
tested Mrs. Bishop. But she was already making for the
davenport.
Mrs. Meigs chuckled. She could see the baby better if
she sat opposite him than if she held him. Just the same
she would rather have held him. If Mrs. Bishop was go-
ing to win that last game of Canfield, she'd have to win
it on her merits.II
MOLLY O'NEIL'S husband had been drowned when
her baby was only two months old. At the time of his
death Dennis was working for his cousin Tip, who mar-
ried Mrs. Meigs's granddaughter. To get Molly out of
Tip's way, Mrs. Meigs had installed Molly in her spare
bedroom. She might, Mrs. Meigs told Tip, help Anna a
little with the housework.
But Anna, who had presided over Mrs. Meigs's
kitchen for several years, announced firmly that there
wasn't any housework around here she couldn't tend to
herself. On the other hand. Mrs. Meigs indignantly re-
fused to let Tip pay Molly's board. Molly stayed on as a
guest. In Mrs. Meigs's eyes, she stayed on as Sonny's
mother.
She was an instinctive mother. Watching her Sunday
morning while she bathed the baby, nursed him, put him
out for his morning nap, Mrs. Meigs warmed to Molly.
She never made a tenth part of the fuss over her baby
that other people made over him; but she had only to
lift him in her round young arms to make you conscious
of the blood tie between them. Her baby was, and
would always remain, a part of herself.
As soon as he was put outdoors for his nap, however,
she sank into apathy. She established herself on the
davenport within reach of a book, the Sunday news-
paper, a pile of stockings she had laid aside to darn.
Then she folded her arms, and sat staring vaguely out
of the window at the wall of the building across the
IS16
A NICE LONG EVENING
street, and a little patch of gray sky. Perhaps she hadn't
yet got over the shock of Dennis's death. Perhaps the
baby was too great a drain upon her strength, though
she looked healthy enough to have a baby every other
year, and live to see her great-grandchildren. But to
Mrs. Meigs's eyes even her undeniable beauty was ob-
scured by a veil of dullness.
She had been invited to Estelle's supper party to-
night, and had refused to go. When Jack arrived in the
middle of the afternoon to take his mother for a ride,
he asked Molly to go with them; but Molly smiled
faintly and shook her head. After all, her apathy had its
advantages for Mrs. Meigs. Suppose Molly had elected
to pay for her keep by being a cheerful companion, or
by following Mrs. Meigs around the house with shoulder
wraps and foot-stools!
The snow had ceased during the night, and the down
town streets had been cleared by the snow plow.
Mrs. Meigs was happy to be going off with her only son
in his car; and as she was alone with him, there was
nothing to stop her sitting in the front seat. A woman
doesn't get many rides alone with a married son.
Nevertheless she remembered to ask politely, "Why
didn't you bring Estelle with you?"
Jack made a face. "Estelle's busy over the damn sup-
per. She's been getting ready for this party for a week.
You'd think she expected the President and his Cabinet,
at least."
"My dear, she expects your mother and sisters, and
that's worse." Mrs. Meigs chuckled, but she added so-
berly, "You've been married years enough, but this is
Estelle's first party in her own home. Naturally she
wants to have everything nice."
"She had me working all morning. It's lucky I could
plead a date with you, or I shouldn't have got off now."A NICE LONG EVENING
17
Jack stopped for a traffic light, in a down town street
that showed a Sunday emptiness. "Any special place you
want to go?”
"Between your car and Edith's and Tip's," said
Mrs. Meigs, "I'm usually taken for an auto ride Sunday
afternoon. We always go to see how the new Civic Audi-
torium is getting along; and then we make a circuit of
the suburbs, and tell each other it's wonderful how
they're building up."
"Just for that," said Jack, "I've a good mind to drive
up and down Grand Avenue until you howl for mercy."
But it was worth while dropping Jack a hint. He took
her through a maze of back streets, until she confessed
herself lost. Then he shot out a highway to the south;
and presently, after losing her again, he turned a corner
that brought the shore of Lake Michigan directly before
them, on the unfashionable but beautiful point of land
which looked across the waters of the bay to down town.
"Why, Jack, this is where we used to come for picnics
when you were a little boy!" said Mrs. Meigs.
"It's inside the city limits now, though it's about six
miles from your apartment," said Jack. "I shouldn't be
surprised if the spot where we picnicked is actually in
the drink by this time. The breakwater hasn't worked
the way they expected it to. Look, here's a street with
only one side to it. Nice beach at the foot of the bluff,
though. Handy for swimming, if it was July instead of
February."
"Well, they've shoveled their walks on the side of the
street that's left," remarked Mrs. Meigs. "Can't we get
out and walk up to the end of the block?"
He balanced her elbow along past a neat row of
bungalows and cottages, each set in its own lawn. They
were all sunk in the torpor of Sunday afternoon follow-
ing a heavy dinner; but obviously they were all occupied18
A NICE LONG EVENING
by the people who owned them. Then at the very end of
the block, in a lot twice the usual size, was a roomy
bungalow with blank windows and a cold chimney. The
walk there had not been shoveled. Jack started to turn
back.
But Mrs. Meigs stood still, with her eyes fixed upon a
sign nailed to the porch. "'For Sale or for Rent," she
read. "Jack, that place would do for a whole household.
There's a full upstairs, I can tell to look at it. I wonder
whether there are two bathrooms?"
"Oh, very likely! It's wonderful how this part of the
city is building up," Jack teased her.
Mrs. Meigs's thoughts flew to her granddaughter, mar-
ried now and keeping house in a one-room apartment.
"I suppose it's a little large for Cecile."
"Cile's lucky to have a roof over her head at all," said
Jack. "Nice fellow, Tip, but hardly the pattern of a
good provider."
Her daughter Natalie's husband wasn't a good pro-
vider either, and Edith and Millicent both owned houses.
But as Mrs. Meigs's eye lingered on the windows of the
bungalow, open on every side to the unobstructed light,
and on the trellis for climbing roses, and on the wide
porch where you could bring out your rocking-chair
of a summer afternoon, and sit and look across the Lake,
which went on and on in tumbling water until it met
the horizon, audacious words leaped to Mrs. Meigs's
tongue.
"I'd like to take that house myself, if I thought I
could swing it," she said. "There'd be a yard for Sonny to
play in. And once more before I die, I'd like to live
in a house where I could go upstairs to bed. I'm used
to just opening a door off the dining-room and crawling
in; but I must say going upstairs always strikes me as
more-well, respectable."A NICE LONG EVENING
19
Jack laughed. "I'll certainly have to have you put
under guardianship one of these days, Mrs. Meigs. It
took a family row to get things fixed so that you could
stay in that apartment of yours. Before my ears have
cooled off from what my sister Millicent said to me
then, here you go wanting to take a house and lot so
that you can have plenty of stairs to climb."
There was a shade of anxiety underneath his teasing.
Of course, at her age it would be ridiculous to take on
further housekeeping responsibilities. Of course too it
wouldn't do to make plans that hinged on Sonny's con-
tinuing indefinitely to live with her. Mrs. Meigs felt
that the baby belonged to her, but Molly showed no
signs of taking root. And Anna might have something
to say about moving so far from the center of town,
and undertaking to keep forty or fifty casement win-
dows clean.
Well anyhow, it was a nice dream. Being eighty didn't
keep you from making fanciful plans, just for fun.
Only it was too bad that nowadays those pleasant im-
possible plans always began, "If I were twenty years
younger." At Jack's age she would have said, "If I had
a million dollars." Never expecting to have the million
dollars, of course; but there's a certain charm in de-
liberately sticking your head up in the clouds. There is
no charm at all in running your head against a stone
wall. Mrs. Meigs was quiet all during the rest of the
drive.
Perhaps it was just as well that she had saved her
breath to admire Estelle's bungalow. Estelle had spent
months getting the place just as she wanted it. It was
filled with lamps that you couldn't see anything by,
and cushions that mustn't be sat against; all the windows
had triple curtains, and if anywhere you were threat-
ened by a vacant space that might rest your eyes, the20
A NICE LONG EVENING
threat was forestalled by a "winter bouquet" of dried
flowers dyed strange colors and berries that had been
shoeblacked.
Mrs. Meigs was as enthusiastic as possible. Estelle's
taste left something to be desired, but she was doing
her best to make a home for Jack. After all, Mrs. Meigs
wasn't always in sympathy with the taste of her own
daughters; she had never quite forgiven Edith for going
modernistic.
Edith was the first of the daughters to arrive. She
was wearing a new costume, which couldn't quite make

ing was miraculous, though her mother wasn't quite able
to see the idea of nails varnished blood color. With her

article in nice husbands, even to his air of having been
"brought along" when the party consisted of his wife's
relatives.
Natalie came next. Natalie was the youngest and
prettiest of Mrs. Meigs's daughters, and the one who had
caused her mother the most anxiety. Seeing her to-day,
and Carter Brooks with her, Mrs. Meigs could scarcely
credit how near they had been to the rocks a year ago.
They were like a honeymoon couple now. Carter even
stayed with Natalie when the rest of the husbands got
together in a corner and started to ask each other, how
was business?
Millicent and Ralph Graham came last. They were a

"Why!" Mrs. Meigs whispered to Ralph. "Aren't Tip
and Cecile coming?"
"Evidently not." Ralph seemed disappointed too. HeA NICE LONG EVENING
21
must have looked forward to seeing his daughter on a

Still it was a good thing sometimes for her to have

Across the table Edith too was trying to look uncon-

A NICE LONG EVENING
of kinship would have been there, of course, if it had

than of any of her other children. She had won more

A NICE LONG EVENING
had heard a soft sound, the strangeness of which struck

It was Molly laughing. In all the months that Molly

But of course Richard didn't think of Molly as a girl

by the girls he liked, Molly might as well be in Mrs.
Meigs's generation.
Richard finally got around to telephone, not to one

if that august lady had come his way. Mrs. Meigs won-

moment. Now that her fear had passed, she hesitated to
put a name to it.III
WHEN she woke Monday morning, the snow was melt-
ing. Well, that was what she had to expect late in
February. Snow is like everything else, for that matter;
nothing in this world stays put. Even when you're
eighty years old, and have successfully asserted your
right to your own independence, and have married off
your granddaughter and buried your oldest friend, life
goes on. The more things you fix, indeed, the more
there are to come unfixed.
But there are some things about which you like to
feel secure. She never took at its face value anything that
Richard said. Anyhow to boys like him the idea of
marriage was always a little ridiculous. Even supposing
he was correct in his assumption that Cecile and Tip had
been "having a fight," there was nothing in that to up-
set Mrs. Meigs. They had had plenty of fights before
they were married. Lots of married couples plumed
themselves on their contentment, when they simply
weren't enough interested in each other to fight.
It was high time she went to see Cecile, however.
Cecile came to her grandmother's two or three times a
week; but it was a month at least since Mrs. Meigs had
been in Cecile's apartment. She didn't need any excuse
for going there; but if Tip had left the car for Cecile's
use to-day, Mrs. Meigs might ask Cecile to run her down
to the lake shore bungalow that had fascinated her yes-
terday. There wasn't the remotest chance that either of
them would take it. But they don't charge you anything
to look at a house; and it would be a fascinating game

2728
A NICE LONG EVENING
to imagine the porch furnished for a summer afternoon,
and pink rambler roses climbing over the newly painted
trellis.
Cecile's apartment was only a few blocks away. After
lunch Mrs. Meigs put on her stoutest pair of rubbers, and
set out. She stopped to do one or two errands, and it
was after three when she rang Cecile's bell. She hadn't
phoned that she was coming; and there was no special
reason to suppose that Cecile would be at home in the
afternoon.
She was at home, however. She was wearing a tan
sweater suit that had seen better days, and was arrang-
ing on new shelves a lot of books that Tip had kept
stacked in corners or shoved under the davenport. He
had lived here before he was married; and though the
kitchen arrangements were nothing but a hole in the
wall, and the chiffonier had to stand in the bathroom,
and the broom and dust mop were kept in the clothes
closet, the room was large and light, and even attractive,
if you didn't object to makeshifts.
"Take Tip's easy chair, Caroline," ordered Cecile.

Mrs. Meigs's heart sank. She wanted Cecile to hold

37
made it a policy; and those women were heads of their

She wished she hadn't told about that ancient grudge

She'd take the street car home; she opened her hand-

SHE hadn't even got around to mention the bungalow.

She was company for Mrs. Meigs at meals, though

That would be the final solution, doubtless; but if
Molly married again she would take the baby with her.

variably forgot them when she could. Time enough to

She caught herself thinking of the lake shore bunga-

would learn to swim as soon as he could walk. And he

time he was, he might not be living with Mrs. Meigs

It was the old story of the tooth that stops aching when
you only think of the dentist.
Dr. Manning telephoned soon after breakfast. He

It might or it might not, reflected Mrs. Meigs. The
Lake was a gorgeous deep blue to-day, with occasional

lived where she could look at Lake Michigan every day,
she probably wouldn't do anything else.

SHE paid a small deposit on the rent, and took his re-
ceipt for it. It was worth ten dollars to let him know
that though she was an old lady in old-fashioned mourn-
ing, she wasn't just walking around to save funeral
expenses.
She would forfeit her deposit, of course, when he
mailed her his precious lease. Her present apartment was
an ideal place for her to live: hadn't she proved that at
length to every member of her family connection, only
last October? The bungalow was remote from all her
usual haunts. It would involve seeing to one's own
heat, and looking after a yard. The very idea was pre-
posterous. She hadn't lived eighty years without learn-
ing when to let well enough alone.
No one need ever know about this fit of insanity, un-
less some day in the distant future she told it as a joke

easier than she deserved, Mrs. Meigs watched strange

SHE was committed now, and she had no intention of

Mrs. Meigs had always abhorred fixed dates. Spending

"You're moving?" said Millicent in tones of awful

A NICE LONG EVENING
"I can move the first of April," said Mrs. Meigs. "Or

"And may I ask why you changed your mind all of

"That sounds as if I was an old stick-in-the-mud,"

The corners of Cecile's mouth twitched. Millicent,

took such an important step, your children might at

"I'm consulting you now," said Mrs. Meigs.
"Tea is ready," said Cecile.
It is difficult to show your righteous indignation over
a cup of tea; and Millicent shared the family liking for
food. When she had silently refreshed herself, however,
Mrs. Meigs was prepared for a real explosion. It would
be just as well to let Millicent have her say and get it
out of her system. There was no denying anyhow that
this time she had a good deal of right on her side.
But when Millicent had finished her tea, she sat for a

really a very busy woman. She'd see Cecile on Sunday.
When she had swept out, Mrs. Meigs smiled at Cecile.
"That was unexpectedly easy. Have I been doing your

73
"It was too easy." Cecile frowned, thinking the thing

It was great fun, this putting heads together with

and Tip were married. When they had been together,
Mrs. Meigs had reminded herself every now and then,
Cecile belonged to Tip now; Grandmother must begin

it wouldn't do for her to come crowding in and-making

A NICE LONG EVENING
light was still luminous above surrounding roofs. The

THOUGH headed off in a sense, thanks to Cecile's
prompt action, Mrs. Meigs's family had plenty to say

Natalie's support was drowned in Jack's growls. "You

thing that I suggested. But I couldn't be satisfied. We
must go to housekeeping. And damn it, she's turned into

"Her zeal will wear off," predicted Mrs. Meigs.

Mrs. Meigs remembered that it was three weeks since
she had entertained her own bridge players. For more
years than she could count, now, every two or three

"having your old ladies."
In the days when bridge was a daring variation of
whist, the four of them had taken turns in entertaining.
But now that Mrs. Bishop lived with her married daugh-

Miss Lamb in the Old Ladies' Home, Mrs. Meigs did all

she had to start dismantling the apartment. She con-

"Half a trick?" faltered Mrs. Bishop. "How can you
take half a trick?"
Mrs. Cronkhite endeavored to explain. Unluckily she
gave the statements on the authority of her own teacher;
she had always derided the opinions of "experts," but
now she named her own expert as if he were her patron
saint.
"Well," said Mrs. Meigs firmly, "if I have six of a suit
with the nine high, I can always bid one in that suit, and
make it, too."
Mrs. Cronkhite put down that heresy with a firm
hand. Such haphazard bidding might do in Auction.
But Contract called for scientific bidding. Only the
proper bidding of the hands would insure success at
Contract.
"Don't you have to play the hands after you bid
them?" asked Miss Lamb in bewilderment.
Mrs. Cronkhite began again at the beginning. She
had all the zeal of the convert; though she didn't callA NICE LONG EVENING
81
it a lecture, she held forth steadily for a quarter of an

"That's going backward, for us," said Mrs. Meigs.
"We've always overbid our hands, without knowing
that we had a system."
Not the ghost of a smile crossed anybody's face.
Maybe that wasn't such a good joke. But for twenty
years they had been laughing at each other's jokes. Now
Miss Lamb was pale with concentration, and Mrs. Bishop
looked as if her nearest and dearest lay at death's door.
"We can play a few rounds now," said Mrs. Cronkhite
graciously. "After each hand, I'll explain the mistakes
in everybody's bidding. That will drive home the points
of what I've been telling you."
It promised to be a lovely afternoon, thought

spades stared at her, with the nine high.
Mrs. Cronkhite, who had dealt, bid a heart. "One
spade," said Mrs. Meigs.
She and her partner went to four spades, and made
five. "Mrs. Meigs, you didn't have an opening bid in

"It counts on the score, though, doesn't it?" said

She closed her ears to the storm which followed. She
had forgotten now that she was going to move her

that was, to beat Mrs. Cronkhite these first two rubbers.

SHE screwed up her courage the next day and told
Mrs. Bishop of her intended move. Mrs. Bishop was
aghast. "You're the only friend I had that I could drop
in on. And now you're going miles away, and goodness

been talking for years about buying a car, but so far
he's never bought one."
"My tribe have cars enough to get you down there,"
said Mrs. Meigs. "And it's far enough away so that when
you are once there, you'll have a good excuse for stay-
ing. I'm going to have a lovely guest-room, and there's
a porch overlooking the Lake. Hot afternoons this com-
ing summer, you and I can just sit and rock, and look
at the water."
"It'll be nice for you. I'll come and see you when I

right across the hall for so many years!"
She was inconsolable over the prospect of her loss. To
make the most of the few weeks when Mrs. Meigs was
still at hand, she took to coming in every afternoon; and

Mrs. Meigs's last move had been from the huge old
house that was the scene of her married life. She still
dreamed sometimes that she was trying to crowd her
88A NICE LONG EVENING

furniture into this apartment, trying to make up her

There was no cause for nightmare this time. Going

were born an accumulator, an accumulator you would
die.
She'd need furniture for one more bedroom, indeed;

skinny chairs and hospital-looking beds that she was put-

There were colors to be selected for the bungalow,

many windows, and flirted with the idea of porch furni-

and since she had decided never to give up her widow's
mourning, she had had to bother very little with new
clothes. But she had always loved to shop for the house.
Having a new house to shop for was great fun, though it
tired her more than she had expected.

And after a day divided between sympathy and shop-

She had supposed that Molly would soon tire of her
self-appointed task. There was no reason why Molly
should be especially interested in the conquest of Canada;
the French names bothered her, and she shuddered at
the gruesome details of Indian massacre and torture. But
she had made up her mind that "hist'ry" was improving

slow progress of Molly's book mark through the first
volume of Mr. Parkman's great work, and then counted
how many other volumes lay ahead, she groaned aloud.
Toward the end of March there was a break in the
routine. The baby had to be weaned completely; and
Cecile, hearing of the event, telephoned to Molly to come
over and spend a week-end with her. Tip was going to
be out of town for four days. Could Molly come Thurs-

In Molly's absence it took all of Mrs. Meigs's time and
Anna's to look after Sonny. No sooner was he bathed

MRS. MEIGS was surprised to find that her moving
was a matter of public concern. The iceman, the news-
boy, the cobbler in the next block, the man who sold
her roast chestnuts, all told her how sorry they would
be to lose her from the neighborhood. It appeared that
the most casual relations came in time to mean some-
thing. She felt guilty at walking out on them all.
When she first committed herself, the end of April
had looked a long way off. It was upon her before she
knew it. The painter varnished himself out of the front
door of the bungalow. The new curtains were delivered,
all ready to hang. Anna packed dishes; and Mrs. Meigs
got the grocery man to save her some wooden boxes that
would do for books. Luckily the movers were expected
to do a lot for you nowadays. She mustn't make a bogey
of her moving.
Mrs. Bishop, coming in upon Molly wrapping books in
newspapers, and Mrs. Meigs marking the boxes with
black crayon, shook her head and sighed. "We've played
our last bridge game in this apartment!" she said dole-
fully.
"I'm afraid we've played our last bridge game any-
where," said Mrs. Meigs. "I don't propose to go tearing
my brains out over Contract."
"You never were a very serious Auction player," said
Mrs. Bishop. "But you always managed to do your share
of the winning.”
A little too much of the past tense, thought
Mrs. Meigs. She looked over to where Molly was wrap-

"I'm going to tell Anna to make the coffee right
away," she announced. "If we go on playing in front
of Dr. Manning, we'll furnish him with too many good
stories about how women play cards."
Anna was polishing spoons at the kitchen table. "I've
let the silver go this week," she apologized. "Want me to
use the electric percolator for a change? The gas stove
is safer, but we'll put on a little style this time, if you
say so."
The familiar tone of every day was heartening.
Mrs. Meigs felt her panic begin to evaporate. Not even
to herself had she admitted what it was that she was
afraid of; and probably there was nothing in it any-

maybe it hadn't been such a good idea to poke this en-
tertaining into days that were too full already.
Her heart was resuming its normal beat when she
started back toward the front of the flat. But in the
confusion of the dining-room, Dr. Manning was wait-
ing for her.
He didn't look like himself without his enormous
toothy grin; and he spoke more quietly than she had
supposed he could speak. "Have you a good strong light
in your bedroom? Come in there for a minute. I want
to have a look at you."106
A NICE LONG EVENING
He still had a physician's authority, when he chose

But when he turned and strode into her bedroom, she

He turned on her bedside light and fiddled with the

She looked at him. She noted that his cuffs were
frayed, and his hair needed cutting. She wondered how
many days he had worn that shirt. There was no need
for any man to look like that. No matter how old he
was, he could still keep himself tidy.
He put a hand on her head, and she shrank a little
from his touch. With his thumb he lifted her eyelid,
and then her other eyelid. His hands were neither im-
maculate nor steady, but he had kept his physician's
touch. He must have had a good bedside manner in his
day. A pity his day was so long gone by. He wasn't an
interesting sort of relic.
He let go her head, and readjusted the lampshade.
"Well?" she asked, with an attempt at her usual im-
pudence.
"How long is it since you saw your oculist?" asked
Dr. Manning.
"Oh, I've known for quite a while that I ought to see
him again! But there are always so many more interest-

"I should advise you to see him-soon," said
Dr. Manning.
His seriousness frightened her. She would have wel-
comed his silly grin now. But she wasn't going to let
herself be afraid of Dr. Manning, or of two Dr. Man-
nings. "I suppose my glasses need changing, or some-

Why hadn't she admitted it sooner? She'd let MollyA NICE LONG EVENING
107
read to her, because reading to herself was increasingly
difficult. The figures on the cards were blurring, too, so
that only the colors were left to distinguish the suits.
But nowadays even children wore glasses; wore them

She'd always prided herself on her good eyesight; yes,
and she had abused it. A visit to the oculist should be
as much a matter of routine as a visit to the dentist.
She could pay either visit, she hoped, without getting

But Dr. Manning still looked unnaturally grave; and

"I can't tell you a thing that you don't know al-

Well, he's a good man."
She turned on her heel, and sailed into the living-

she shouldn't have listened to him in the first place.
He was a silly old man, who talked with his mouth
full and ogled them all impartially, and showed off like
a fool, just because he was the only man in a roomful
of women.108
A NICE LONG EVENING
Dr. Manning indeed! It would be worth moving, just

This was the last party she would ever give in the
old apartment. Everybody was so amiable and so

Molly ought to get home in time to give them another

It was the last of many parties, and in some ways the
best. It should have made a pleasant recollection for

old apartment, Mrs. Meigs? We always had such good
times together there, didn't we?" It should have been
something to talk over afterward, as now and then they
did talk over the best of their good times.
But the rest of that afternoon passed like a night-
mare.X
EDITH was to send over her car Friday morning, to
take down anything that Mrs. Meigs considered too
precious to trust to the movers. But instead of leaving
this important business to the chauffeur, she came her-
self, and brought Natalie with her.
Mrs. Meigs selected the clock that had been her hus-
band's present to her on their twenty-fifth anniversary,
a china shepherd and shepherdess that had belonged to

a funny little gingham hat that Richard had worn when
he was just learning to walk. She was embarrassed by
having to make a choice; she liked all the things that
she was accustomed to, and in spite of her brave words

Edith drifted through the rooms, pointing out to the

giving him his cup of milk. She made a charming picture

She had always been the prettiest of Mrs. Meigs's daugh-
ters; Edith's good looks were largely grooming, and
Millicent was too commanding to be called anything
less than "handsome."
The car was finally loaded, and they drove down to
the bungalow. The Lake was as smooth as glass to-day,
and a lovely green in color. "It's never the same two
days, they say," murmured Natalie. "Think of living
here, Mother, and watching it change!"

He saved her the trouble. She had been right, of course.
It was an easy guess at her age, when everything dimmed,
and new glasses would only "help." Yet when the verdict

that moment, she had not given up hope.XI
IT was cataract. A little film thickening between you
and the world, until familiar pursuits became impossible,
and dear faces faded, and at length the bright light of
day itself was shut out. Cataract! The thing old people
dread, as the middle-aged dread cancer. Yet until yester-
day she had never dreaded it consciously, had never
thought of it in relation to herself. She had always prided
herself on her good eyesight: had simply expected to go
on seeing well, right up to the end.
If the end had been death, she would have faced it
composedly, almost cheerfully. After eighty, your own
death is no great calamity. She didn't want to leave the
bright amusing spectacle of life; it would be a bitter
wrench to part from her children, and worse still to leave
her grandchildren. But the fingers that grip life so eagerly
when you are twenty slacken as you grow old. Her hus-
band's death had made dying easier to her. Only lately
Cal Fletcher had gone, and taken with him the last
remnant of her vanished youth. Death at least is final;
and when the bitterness of the idea has grown familiar
to you, you realize, the dead alone are safe. For a moment
she envied them.
But she had to put that out of her mind. She steadied
herself to learn just what lay ahead of her. For all her
vague dread of this very thing that had happened to her,
she knew very little about it. She had to learn more, if
only to keep other people from learning.
She impressed that on Dr. Burns to begin with. He

mustn't tell Jack. None of her children must find out

A NICE LONG EVENING
"But how long is that going to take? A month? Two

"We can't tell exactly how long it will take. We are

He went on to speak of periodic examinations. He
diverged to her general health. He complimented her on

Her courage! She was quaking like a jelly inside. She
sat and listened to him now, only because she was afraid
that if she cut him short and rose to leave, her legs
wouldn't hold her up. Yet she heard everything he told
her; she weighed and valued it, and knew that what en-
couragement he gave her was only sugar coating.
There was no certainty that an operation would give
her back her sight. He carefully avoided promising that;
he would go no farther than "good hopes." But even
suppose he had been able to promise her restored sight:
what was restored sight at the end of three or four years,
when they were three or four years after eighty?
Even at eighty she had proposed to carry on; but she
knew that she carried on now with the odds against her.
Every year would see her more frail, less able to resist
or rally. Every year now was not an added burden: it
was twenty added burdens. Yet here he came asking her
to let the years pile up now, on the slim chance that she
might outwit them in the end!
She went on questioning him, in the face of a growing
conviction that he had already told her all he knew, andA NICE LONG EVENING
121
her guess was as good as his, when it came to guessing.

Mrs. Meigs rose slowly. Yes, her legs would still support

"Toric lenses and no rims, I think we decided," said

"I'll come in and get them one of these days," said

She would never have planned to leave, if she had

persuade herself, a thing was no worse because you knew
about it. She had insisted on knowing; she must learn
to bear her knowledge.
She refused the nurse's offer of a taxicab. But in the
street she found herself dazed. Twilight was falling;
lamps were springing out of the mist. Or was there a
mist? Wasn't it just that she couldn't see that out of
the dimness which she carried with her now, looming
shapes might rise before she recognized them, and strike
her down?
You weren't safe, if you couldn't see. She hesitated
in the door of the building. The doorman looked at her
curiously. She could see that all right.
"Any chance of picking up a taxi here?" she asked. "I
didn't want to wait for them to phone for one."
The doorman stowed her in a taxi. What an idiot she
2.122
A NICE LONG EVENING
had been, to reject the nurse's offer in the first place! She
must get over that false pride of hers, must take advantage

She didn't want the kind of sympathy that failing

on her life even now so that every day seemed a victory.
It wasn't a valiant Mrs. Meigs who cowered in a corner
of the taxicab, shading her eyes from the lights that

could just get home and get to bed! The vision of her

cool sheets, glimmered before her; it seemed to her like
an island of safety, where horrors could not follow nor
find her.
Yet the taxi was jerking her home almost too fast. If
she chose to come home in a taxi, no one had the right
to question her. But she wished she could rouse herself
to lean forward and rap on the glass: simply tell the
driver to set her down at the next corner, and walk the
remaining distance home. The few blocks would have
meant nothing to her a week ago. Now the effort was
impossible.
The taxi reached her own block, and slowed down.
Mrs. Meigs fumbled for her handkerchief, and hastilyA NICE LONG EVENING
123
wiped her forehead. There was no light in her living-

She reached the shelter of her own room. Anna heard

"He's in a class by himself," said Mrs. Meigs. Her

Anna set up a folding table in the living-room, and

While she was at dinner, the real estate agent tele-

She was giving a good imitation of herself so good
that once or twice she too was carried away by it, and
found herself eagerly planning a summer with lots of124
A NICE LONG EVENING
guests, a summer of lake winds and sunshine. You never
got enough outdoors in an apartment.
When warm weather came they might cut to two

all they'd need would be a cold supper at six; and a cold

"Yeah, it'll be almost like living at a summer cottage,"
said Anna. "When I used to work for Mis' Bixby, and

a free boarding house all summer for her children's and

didn't notice her staying."
"You'll have your Sunday afternoons off as usual,
Anna, if you care to take them," said Mrs. Meigs. "And
the guests will do the supper dishes. I'll see to that."
She loved making plans. Now the wild ideas which
had swept through her head that February Sunday when
Jack first drove her down to see the bungalow, were all
on the point of realization. Now she'd even have a good
excuse for staying at home Sundays; she had suffered too
long from her children's fixed idea that the proper way
to spend Sunday afternoon was to take Mother for an
auto ride.
She ate less dinner than usual; but the mere taste of
food, the smell of coffee, the feel of her own rocking-
chair and the glow of her living-room with all the lamps
lighted as usual for the evening, helped to banish her
sensation of nightmare. The news this afternoon had
given her a physical shock; that at least was wearing off.
But physical comfort was the smaller half of the secur-
ity she was accustomed to. Much more important was
feeling snug in her mind. She had always despised people
who kept a careful watch on their own emotions; theyA NICE LONG EVENING
125
were as much invalids in their way as the poor souls who
were always counting their own pulse or taking their own
temperature. To keep busy and not to think too much
about yourself: that was the way to keep your mind
serene.
That had been her way these many years. But now her
feeling of security had gone, and her means of getting
outside herself were threatened. The lovely Lake would
lie spread out where she could look at it every day-and
every day she would be less able to see its loveliness. The
baby, every step of whose progress she had watched with
such interest-the baby, who smiled when he saw her,
who would go to her away from anybody but his mother
-the baby was getting to the place now where he needed
an ever more alert attention. And the baby would gradu-
ally fade before her loving eyes.
She mustn't borrow trouble. It might be a long time
yet before she was reduced to helplessness. Yet it would
be three or four years before they could relieve her, the
oculist had said and there was no certainty that they
could help her then. Until then, she must stumble and
grope increasingly, more and more dependent on other
people, losing her interests one by one, until at last the
summer sunshine, the lovely light of day that came in
such floods down in the open places where she was going
-the very sunshine would be no more to her than it
would to a lizard who might crawl out of his hole in an
old wall to feel the warmth on his back.
She was to move to-morrow morning. Now that the
great undertaking was so close, fresh details would re-
quire to be settled. New plans kept springing up in her
mind. She spoke of them with something of her usual
eagerness. A good habit is as hard to break as a bad one.
But in between times the sickening realization would
come back. Now that the first shock was over, the return126
A NICE LONG EVENING
was less a nightmare than a numbness. Already she must
be getting used to it.
She would give half her remaining years to keep the

commonplace interests of every day, Mrs. Meigs grew

"Are you packing to-night?" asked Mrs. Meigs.

This afternoon in the circulating library Mrs. Meigs

She realized that she was keeping Molly waiting. "You

She had drawn her favorite rocking-chair to the im-

127
the yawning book shelves, at the cleaner patches on the

shrank a little, as if memory had stabbed her. But you

When they began those readings, it had been with the

Now for this simplest of pleasures she would be de-
pendent on some one else. Now all her reading would have
to be calculated and arranged for. Getting old amounted
to having your life chipped away from you a little at
a time. Still enough had remained, up to now. But to be
old and blind-
The word was like a knife thrust into her throat, cut-
ting off her breath. For hours now she had shrunk from
using that word, even to herself. But in the secret places
of her mind, it had come to stay. When a dog was old
and blind, you put him out of the way, and counted it
a kindness. But no one would do that much for her.
She was tired all over, so tired that her flesh was sore;A NICE LONG EVENING
129
and even leaning against the high back of her rocking-

had gleamed before her as a refuge when she was far

With such a hard day ahead of her, she ought to lie
down and rest, even if she couldn't go to sleep. Sleep

tired, so tired. But she couldn't go to sleep with a light

To-night it would not be the kindly dark which brings
slumber. It would be the pitiless dark which meant only
one thing, the encroaching dark which must now remind
her that, whether it came slowly or fast, every minute
that passed was bringing her closer to the time when
darkness and light would be all one to her.XII
NEXT morning she was still so weary that she would
have liked to stay in bed until noon, turning a deaf ear
even to offers of breakfast. Instead she had to get up and
move.
She was spared the actual work in connection with her
moving. Millicent was back now from her convention in
the East, and Millicent would rather have missed a wed-
ding than a move. Edith's chauffeur drove Mrs. Meigs
down to the bungalow right after breakfast, so that she

the book-store, passed around cigarettes, the affair seemed
less like a move than like a picnic.
But the moving lasted most of the day; and evening
found Mrs. Meigs in a strange place, with her possessions
in such confusion that even when, after much search-

to her. The sloping lines of the roof cut one wall of her
new bedroom, too, so that if her bureau stood where it
would get the best light, it had to stand two feet out
from the wall. And most of her electrical appliances
wouldn't work here; the old apartment had been in a
down town district which still used direct current, but
the bungalow was on alternating.
Richard came down on Sunday to see about the electri-

new one, he said. The toaster and the percolator and the

This was the line all the young people took. It seemed
to them that Mrs. Meigs was making a fine brave start
at new living. She had taught them what to expect from
her; and in their eyes she had surpassed herself now. But
what seemed to them a splendid reaching out for new
years of happiness, had turned in her own mind to a
ghastly mockery.
Still she fell in with the new plans that were made.
She had Cecile order three rattan rocking-chairs for the
front porch. She let Richard sell her, at a jobber's dis-
count, a tea wagon that you plugged in anywhere, and
it would almost order the meals for you, it was so com-
plete. Tip engaged Molly to meet him down town this132
A NICE LONG EVENING
week, and pick out a new bed for the baby; and
Mrs. Meigs directed them to get a chest of drawers to

Life was going on, and she might as well go with it.
On Monday she walked the three blocks to the neighbor-
hood where the drug-store and the movie and the queer
little jewelry shop made up a sort of small down town.
Mrs. Meigs was used to living within walking distance
of the real down town, and she was going to miss it. But
in a way these neighborhood settlements made you feel
right at home. Mrs. Meigs stocked up on toilet soap, and
in the jewelry store she bought a hand-painted plate that
would have to go to the bottom of the pile before it was
a week old. That night she took Molly down to see the
picture show.
Movies were a much maligned amusement. They were
easier for Mrs. Meigs to see than things closer at hand.
Molly enjoyed the comedies and was very respectful
toward the animal pictures and the travel talks. Mrs. Meigs
preferred the histories of cabaret singers, and private
secretaries who married the boss. Their goings-on startled
her at times; they seemed a queer thing to show to the
neighborhood housewives, and to the high-school children
who came flocking into the theater in giggling droves, or
very sedately boy and girl together. But Mrs. Meigs was
interested on the whole in the scenes of gilded vice which
flickered before her on the screen. They had at least
the charm of novelty for her, and she couldn't learn
younger.
As the bungalow gradually fell into order, the weather
grew warmer and brighter. A second Sunday brought the
young people to supper, with the arrangements made in
advance. And Monday morning the new chairs were de-
livered, and lined up on the porch.
Early in the afternoon, Mrs. Meigs seated herself in theA NICE LONG EVENING
133
lowest of the new chairs, without even a magazine for
company. She had moved the chair into the sunshine;
basking in the warmth, she rocked, and looked at the
Lake, and something of her old-time serenity stole over

Living to her had always meant activity. But perhaps
life still had a lesson in store for her. Perhaps mere living
had some deep significance of its own, which she would
come in time to appreciate and even to enjoy.
She was beginning to feel rested from the move. The
brightness of the spring day, the beauty spread out be-
fore her, were excuse enough not to do anything. And
the threat to her independence, which was one of the
worst consequences of failing eyesight, had not yet been
made good. Worse might lie ahead. It undoubtedly did.
But this afternoon she was going to sit here and be happy,
and no one on earth could prevent her.
Three small boys, bent on daring the iciness of water
that wouldn't be warm for two months yet, slid down the
bank to the beach, at the peril of their necks; to go by
the flight of steps would have taken them a block out of
their way, and have deprived them of the chance to shriek
all the way down. A policeman sauntered by and touched
his cap. A neighbor from the middle of the block came
along wheeling her baby carriage, and smiled at Mrs.
Meigs, and then decided to speak.
Mrs. Meigs hadn't supposed she would ever again be
so contented. She was greedy of all the happiness she
could get; she must store it up, like a reservoir, against
the dark days to come.
Indeed as the sun warmed her, and the May breeze
came fresh over miles of clear water, and the Lake
changed almost moment by moment, but was ever the
same in its fullness of beauty, she found herself recalling
with a start what the something was that always hung134
A NICE LONG EVENING
now like a cloud on her horizon. This was the first time

She leaned her head against the back of her chair.
She'd never been able to doze in a chair; but perhaps
that too was one of the things she had still to learn. She
almost dozed now, but she held her drowsiness at bay,
so that she shouldn't lose a minute of this enchanted after-

was only May, and early May at that. This summer was
a pause before the storm. This summer she meant to en-
joy to the full.
Eve may have been sitting in a rocking-chair when the
serpent first entered Eden; in her innocence she may have
wondered what the new animal was with the pretty

She knew this must be the man the real estate agent

"Nice day, mum," said the man in the black shirt.
"A very nice day," agreed Mrs. Meigs.

135
ain't much about roses I don't know," he informed her.
"When I was in the Philippines-you ever been in the

If he'd spring to his digging, that would be more to

He had worked around the officers' gardens, it ap-
peared; but that was later, when he joined the regulars.
The first year in the Philippines, he'd been along when
Funston captured Aguinaldo. Mrs. Meigs had maybe read

Mrs. Meigs had; but that didn't get her out of listening

prived of his audience, was setting slowly and clumsily

She had been cheated out of her golden afternoon; and

didn't sound a bit too loud. It was heavenly. And to think
of all the years she'd wasted, shut up in an apartment!
That one rainy day was succeeded by a week of fine
weather. It was unusually warm for May. Anna, after
setting her mint and chives, and sowing parsley seed, had
gone into a fever of gardening. The days when Weeks
was not there, she spaded in the back yard and planted
peas and lettuce. She promised the family rare treats
later on, when her vegetables should be ready to harvest.
Just now meals were about half as good as usual, and often
late at that.
Mrs. Meigs teased her about "gardening fever." But
she was glad to see how Anna was taking to their new
existence. Anna had been with her for years, and
Mrs. Meigs would have been lost without her.
She might close her eyes to the idiosyncrasies of her
"help." She couldn't very well put her fingers in her
ears. Weeks was supposed to give her only half his time;
but his other customers must miss him.
Mrs. Meigs was ready at last to be a proper old lady in
a rocking-chair; and the rocking-chairs waited in a row,
and the weather was miraculous. But let her appear on
the porch, and Weeks at once discovered that the front
lawn needed mowing, or the front walk must be swept.138
A NICE LONG EVENING
He would appear with the lawn mower or the broom; he
was a heavy man, and he needed something to lean on.
Securely propped, he would begin to talk.
He took her through the Cuban affair, and the cam-
paign in the Philippines. He told her about his six
months' sojourn in a Soldiers' Home. "I could 'a stayed
there and been took care of all my life. But I'd ruther
work," he informed her. He told her about going to
Kansas as a harvest hand one summer, and about getting
mixed up with the "wobblies" in Portland, Oregon. He
retold it all, too, though so confused was his method of
narration that Mrs. Meigs could never be sure whether
she was getting the same story or a new one.
Molly looked fairly frightened at his eloquence. When
she saw him, she fled indoors. Anna talked back to him,
and provoked him to occasional fits of gallantry, in which
he smirked at her and threw out hints that if he was "a
marryin' man—"
Mrs. Meigs tried not to listen. But she had never ac-
quired the habit of not listening; and there was an awful
fascination in the way Weeks could keep it up. She had
thought she knew some talkative people before. She had
thought she knew some bores. Well!
She carried out her threat of interviewing the real estate
agent. But he told her Weeks was the best man in the
neighborhood; he didn't drink, and he always showed up
when he said he would. Mrs. Meigs couldn't make the
agent see her point: Weeks didn't cut his grass. Indeed
she was secretly amazed at how annoyed she felt. Per-
haps the overhanging cloud that shadowed her days now
deepened her grievance at being made the target of so
much Philippine reminiscence. If they were cutting off
your leg without anaesthetic, you'd feel a mosquito bite
just the same, and you'd resent it ten times as much.
She took to going for walks on these fine afternoons;A NICE LONG EVENING
139
unluckily the movie theater wasn't open afternoons ex-
cept at the week-end. She must invite her "old ladies"

together. If she confused the cards again, some one would
discover that she wasn't seeing well; and she shrank from
that discovery, as the beginning of the end.
Another week-end went by, and still the marvelous
weather continued. Mrs. Meigs nerved herself and made
a trip to the oculist's office for her new glasses.
She lunched down town and did some shopping after-
ward. She was tired when she got home. Without going
indoors to take off her wraps, she sank into a chair on

There were advantages in living so far from the center
of town; when you got home, it felt all the homier to you.
for the long trip you'd had. Mrs. Meigs threw back her
coat and untied the strings of her bonnet. The Lake was
glittering and in motion to-day; only far out did it
appear quiet, where it went to meet a quiet sky.
The corners of her mouth began to spread. In sheer
enjoyment of the peace and beauty of the moment, she
was smiling to herself as she sat alone.
It was too good to last. She heard the clatter of a lawn
mower at the side of the house and rose to flee. But she
wasn't quick enough. A head loomed over the porch rail-
ing, and a tireless voice boomed, "Oh, I wanted to see you
a minute, mum! Me and Anna have always been good.
friends, but she takes advantage the minute my back is
turned."
He had come to complain because Anna had planted
bachelor's buttons in a space he had reserved for spinach.
Mrs. Meigs told him in no uncertain tones that anything.
Anna wanted planted must be planted. She herself pre-140
A NICE LONG EVENING
ferred to grow flowers; they could buy all the vegetables
they needed.
She had a wild hope that he would lose his temper and
quit. The grass might grow up to the eaves, for all she
cared. Indeed she went so far as to ask him whether he
wasn't neglecting his other customers, coming to her
house almost every day.
He assured her that for a man who loved work as he
loved it, the more customers he had, the harder he worked
for all of them. Mrs. Meigs couldn't remember afterward
how he made the transition from his own industriousness
to the shooting of General Lawton. But the next thing she
knew, the flood was on.
She didn't sit down; but she couldn't quite make up
her mind to turn her back in the middle of a sentence. She
was clinging to the back of a chair for support, and her
head was swimming, when she heard a hearty halloo from
the street.
Her back stiffened at the sound, and too late she re-
gretted not having fled while flight was still possible.
For up the block, with his inevitable umbrella hung over
his arm, and his teeth and spectacles gleaming, came Dr.
Manning.
"I've been waiting for an invitation to visit you in your
new home," he bellowed. "But I didn't get it, so I came
anyhow."
There was nothing to do but ask him to sit down. "I
must be about the first company you've had here," he
remarked cheerfully.
She started to tell him that Richard came down every
Sunday now, and Tip and Cecile. He didn't listen to her.
"Living in the backwoods agrees with you," he boomed.
"You're as blooming as a rose. Or maybe you're an orchid
now, living on fresh air and sunlight. Ha, ha, ha!"
The last time she had seen him, he had been the prophetA NICE LONG EVENING
141
of her doom. She was amazed to discover he was still the
same old Dr. Manning. As if she hadn't suffered from
enough talkativeness this past fortnight, he was opening

street car ride to see her, she supposed she'd have to ask
him to stay to dinner.
Weeks had ceased to lean on the handle of his lawn
mower and was pushing it languidly from the house to
the sidewalk. Dr. Manning's eye rested on the swathe that
Weeks had cut, and his loud gallantry ceased suddenly.
"What's that fellow doing about the dandelions?" he de-
manded.
"Letting them grow," said Mrs. Meigs. "The lawn
mower doesn't discourage them much."
"Doesn't damage 'em a particle," said Dr. Manning.
"Sulphate of iron is the only thing that will get rid of
dandelions. I suppose he hasn't got any?"
"I'm quite sure he hasn't," said Mrs. Meigs.
Dr. Manning watched in unwonted silence as Weeks
cut another languid path. Then he rose suddenly. "There's
a drug-store in the neighborhood, isn't there?" he asked.
"How far will I have to walk?"
An hour later Molly returned from giving the baby his
afternoon ride. Her eyes widened as she came up the
walk. Weeks was squatting on the lawn, hitching his
bulk awkwardly from one dandelion plant to another,
dropping poison on each. Over him, regulating the
amount of each dose, and pointing out any plant that the
unlucky Weeks might have hoped to escape, stood a
thorough tyrant in the person of Dr. Manning.
The dandelion hunters worked their way around the
side of the house. A great peace descended. Molly wheeled
the carriage in the house and returned with the baby.
She put him on a blanket on the porch floor and herself
sat down in the rocking-chair next to Mrs. Meigs's.142
A NICE LONG EVENING
The sun was sinking now. The eastern sky took on
lovely tones of green and violet, which were reflected in
the darkening Lake. Molly began to sing to herself, half
under her breath.
It was a simple little song that she might have learned
when she was a child, something about love, and coming
back. Mrs. Meigs wasn't familiar with it, and she couldn't
make out all the words. But as Molly sat singing to her-
self, in the peace of the dying afternoon, Mrs. Meigs
looked at her with a new, acute interest.
She had suddenly realized that Molly was no longer in
her eyes just the person who brought the baby in. She had
got very fond of Molly herself.XIII
JUNE UNE came, and the roses were halfway up the trellises,
and thickly budded. On sunny afternoons people turned
out in bathing suits, though until after the Fourth of
July Lake Michigan is cold enough to discourage the
hardiest swimmer. Of an evening from her bedroom win-
dow Mrs. Meigs could count the beach parties by the
bonfires twinkling on the shore below.
Dr. Manning had become her most constant visitor.
When Weeks got the lawn to his liking, the seat of opera-
tions was transferred to the back yard. Dr. Manning
would interrupt the shooting of General Lawton to bel-
low, "Don't just skim off the surface that way! Stick
your spade straight down!" The account of how Weeks
stormed and captured San Juan Hill, apparently single-
handed, was drowned in shouts of "Get a line and two
pegs and measure those rows! You can't get a row of
plants straight by your eye!"
Weeks sulked, but he worked. Dr. Manning planted
the back yard with long rows of annuals, leaving a bare
quarter for experiments with vegetables. "You don't
want to bother much with perennials in a rented place,"
he informed Mrs. Meigs. The arrangements were taken
out of her hands, but she couldn't quarrel with the re-
sults. The lawn was like green velvet, and there were
garden flowers for the table every day.
Once or twice she had considered having Mrs. Bishop
down for a week-end; but week-ends were always
crowded with the young people. The second week in
143144
A NICE LONG EVENING
June, however, she got around to "having her old ladies."
It was a sparkling day of early summer when she

TWICE that night, after she had officially retired,
Miss Lamb came paddling down the hall in her curl pa-
pers and her borrowed nightgown, which was at least
six inches too short for her. She felt as guilty as Lady Mac-
beth, though to Mrs. Meigs the episode suggested two
little girls who had been caught whispering by the
teacher.
Next morning Mrs. Meigs urged her to stay on until
Monday; without intending a pun she argued, "You
might as well be hung for an old sheep as for a lamb."
But Miss Lamb insisted on going back to face the
music. "I don't know what the Superintendent will say,"
she announced with a shiver.
Later in the week she called up to report, "She didn't
say anything, and of course I didn't say anything." But
her tone was woebegone. A scolding is over and for-
gotten; but an accusing silence can last indefinitely.
Mrs. Meigs took to having her down for week-ends.
Week-ends now were always busy times. Dr. Manning
usually bossed Weeks on Saturday, and stayed for dinner.
Sundays Richard and Tip and Cecile came down for
swimming and a cold supper. Tip and Cecile sometimes
played golf on Sunday on a public course; that meant
getting up before dawn to get started, and by afternoon
they were both perishing for sleep. But instead of taking
a nap, they would plunge into the icy waters of Lake
Michigan. Tip might not be a fiend for hard work, but
he was with his generation in taking his amusement
strenuously.
ISS156
A NICE LONG EVENING
Richard went home to sleep Saturday and Sunday
nights. "I can't very well ask Miss Lamb to sleep in the
coal bin," he explained. But during the week he often
came straight from business. He would swim before
dinner, and tease Molly and Mrs. Meigs at the table,
and nap or interrupt afterward when Molly read
aloud.
He refused to sleep, however, to an accompaniment
of Indian massacres; and that summer Parkman gathered
dust on the shelf, and Molly learned the way to a new
circulating library. Half her selections Richard rejected
on the title alone. Of others he would say, after a chapter
or two, "You can finish that to yourself to-morrow, when
I'm not here." But some evenings he settled down con-
tentedly, rousing himself only to order, "You can skip
the next page or two," or "It's easy guessing what hap-
pens next. But go on anyhow."
Molly obeyed orders. Mrs. Meigs didn't rebel openly.
When she got tired of listening, she could always fall
back on looking at Molly. Molly was wearing plain white
dresses this summer; they were extraordinarily becoming
to her clear skin and black hair. Now that she had got
over the shock of Dennis's death, she was blooming into
fresh loveliness. Perhaps love and motherhood and be-
reavement will do more for a woman's looks anyhow
than tennis or broiling on the sand. Hordes of stringy
sun-tanned girls passed the bungalow every day, now
that the beach season was fully on; not one of them could
hold a candle to Molly.
Mrs. Meigs was beginning to feel that she had invented
Molly. Then with human inconsistency she went back
on her own invention. One Sunday afternoon when Tip
and Cecile and Richard had raced off to the beach,
carrying a huge new water ball to play with, Mrs. Meigs
turned on Molly, who had watched their departure fromA NICE LONG EVENING
157
the shelter of the porch and then sat down between Mrs.

"Isn't it about time you were getting a bathing suit?"

"The procession is big enough without me," said Molly.
"You can go on a cloudy day, if you like. We get

Molly shook her head. "It isn't the sunshine bothers me.

Mrs. Meigs patted her hand in sympathy. Molly never
alluded to Dennis; but that wasn't the same as saying

Just the same, Molly had no business sitting here with

Tip joined the party on the porch; his expression dared
