Molly nodded. Tip's grandmother had been Dennis's

"My grandmother was a soft-hearted woman, and fed

maybe she gave them money and a kind word. And peo-
ple were always telling her that she shouldn't."
"People always do tell you that," struck in Mrs. Meigs.
"Well, hard times came on. This was long before I was
born, but many's the time I've heard her tell the tale.
Our little town was hit, as the whole country was hit.
My grandfather was out of work, but they'd plenty to
eat, and she went on feeding people that came to the
door. But sometimes she'd think maybe she shouldn't. She
couldn't tell how long the hard times would keep up,
and maybe she was taking from her own family to give
to strangers."
Molly slipped into the house. Doubtless she had heard
the tale before; and mention of the old times and the
old people took her back too powerfully.
But the rest of them were listening as Tip went on,
"One day a young woman came to the door, taking orders

to-order corset in her life. This was in the '90's,

the children crying, and went in and found her."
"Not dead?" gasped Miss Lamb.
Tip nodded. "Starved. She hadn't asked anybody for

"They were not," said Tip. "My grandfather came

with a lot of McCarthys and Daughertys, who came from
New England, where an Irish name doesn't mean a thing.
On the strength of my name I get a chance to contribute
to a lot of Catholic charities; but you can't judge a rose,
you know, even when it's got a name like O'Neil."
Tip and Dr. Manning got into a discussion of why an
Irish name didn't mean anything in New England. The

than usual by the summer sun, looked almost white
against the brown of her skin.
It was hard even to think of her as grown-up, ridicu-
lous to consider her a married woman. Yet the instant

him she smiled, as if it had been days instead of minutes

leaned against his bare legs, like a runner touching goal.162
A NICE LONG EVENING
Their Saturday evening parties weren't as large as

They'd grow again in the fall, probably. But Cecile was

The submissive wife was supposed to be characteristic
of Mrs. Meigs's generation; but Mrs. Meigs herself had
never got to the place where she could forgive the mouse
that spins. She had been largely responsible for Cecile's
marriage to Tip; it had succeeded beyond her hopes. Yet
the result sometimes made Mrs. Meigs a little blue. The
carelessness of youth, once gone, can never be retrieved.
She hated seeing it go quite so soon.
Cecile was settled now. Molly had bloomed amazingly
in the last few weeks, but hers was the pale bloom that
comes out in shadow. Even Tip had lost something of his
irresponsibility.
But Mrs. Meigs could always count on Richard. He

in the store a long time, and it didn't look as if anybody
else was going to buy it. He invited Miss Lamb to take a
stroll on the beach with him at ten o'clock.

"Business doesn't seem to have damped your spirits,"

Mrs. Meigs went to the kitchen, to make sure that

A NICE LONG EVENING
the porch and waited for the spectacle which Richard

Presently there was a glow far out where the water
met the sky. An arc of light appeared, and then the moon
came up indeed, so swiftly that you could almost see
it come, so red at first that it looked like a ship on fire.
When it was clear of the horizon it began to turn from
red to gold; and across the dark water appeared a path
so solid it looked as if you could fairly walk up it.
A full moon, and August was almost here. With the
next full moon, the evenings would begin to be a little
chilly. Through Mrs. Meigs's mind drifted a sentence
from a book Molly had lately been reading to her, "The
moon of the moose is followed by the moon of the
caribou, and then by the moon of the white-tailed deer."
That sounded beautiful, even if you remembered that
it was not a nature lover's program, but the Indian's
memorandum of his food supply. The moon of the moose
was September; so the moon of the white-tailed deer
would usher in Mrs. Meigs's second six months as the
tenant of the bungalow.
She was thoroughly at home here now. She couldn't
be happy, the way things had turned. Perhaps she
wouldn't ever be happy again. The promise of relief was
too far in the future, and too uncertain. At her age, you
couldn't make yourself promises that would come due
three or four years from now.
Might as well face that once for all, and be done with
it. Life grants no armistice to gray hairs, nor to deeds
accomplished; guarantees no last stretch of smooth water
before you make port. The older you grow, the more
subject you are to illness, and to what are pleasantly
termed "failing faculties."
Even if you keep some measure of health, you see
your old friends vanish from the scene inevitably, oneA NICE LONG EVENING
165
by one; until if in mere stubbornness or by the accident
of good arteries, you still hang on, you become simply a
curiosity. You, Caroline Meigs, who used to be famous
because you gave so many parties and played bridge on
a system all your own, and could see a joke farther off
than anybody else could see it even you dwindle at last
to the point where you're remarkable only for the length
of time that you've cluttered up the landscape.
Yet somewhere along the way you learn that no dis-
aster is final. You get used even to bad luck. The news
that had sent her sick and shivering from Dr. Burns's
office a few months ago was now an integral part of her
life. She still waked in the night sometimes with a feeling
of black horror, and couldn't think what the trouble
was, and then remembered, and faced it all afresh, with
clenched fists and bitten lips.
Yet here she was, watching the moon rise. If not pre-
cisely happy, she was certainly contented. It had been a
beautiful day, with her beloved grandchildren here, and
sunshine and fun and friends. To-morrow she was going
to have a look at the garden, and see what Anna and
Weeks were doing with their time.

IT was brutal that this had to happen to Molly. Richard
was always attracted to every pretty face that came his
way-and to some that weren't really pretty, only flip-
pant. Molly was lovely, and Molly was different. Richard
had spent a lot of time at Mrs. Meigs's this summer-more
than even his well-known devotion to his grandmother
would account for.
After what Molly had been through, of course his
gaiety and nonchalance appealed to her. But she should
have known better than to take him seriously. Molly was
a widow with a child; Richard still had a year of college
ahead of him. According to the calendar, indeed, Molly
was a year younger than Richard. But on her own
acknowledgment, she didn't belong with him and his
like.
Well, there was nothing Mrs. Meigs could do to ease
the situation. She had planned to enjoy every minute of
this summer; but now she caught herself counting the
days to September. For Richard would be going back to
college in the fall; and she might hope that absence would
do its work, for Molly as well as for him.
Richard went off on a business trip that next week.
On his way out of town he stopped to inform Mrs. Meigs
that he had an expense account now, and he knew some-
body in every town where he had to stop, so he guessed
the trip wouldn't be too dreadful. He was driving-
Oh, yes! Trains were too slow for him. Did she want to
ride along for a while, and take the Interurban back?

Mrs. Meigs declined the invitation. Then she bit her

Mrs. Meigs was on tenter-hooks until the middle of

"A beautiful ride," said Molly.
She didn't volunteer any further information; but

be resting, of course. But she had merely glanced at the
baby when she came in; she hadn't so much as asked
whether he'd been a good boy. To Mrs. Meigs it seemed
a very long hour; and she was no wiser at the end.
Well, she had a breathing-space that week; Richard
was away for eight days. When he returned he tele-
phoned; but he didn't come down to swim, and he was
apparently gratifying his mother by sleeping at home
for a few nights.

come down here many times; she hadn't ever come alone.

the street car.
Millicent wouldn't give up this much of her time unless
she was after something. Could Dr. Burns have told her
what Mrs. Meigs had been trying to keep from every-
body?
Or perhaps Dr. Manning had talked, and the news had170
A NICE LONG EVENING
spread, as bad news will. In that case, Mrs. Meigs would

If Weeks is in the garden, you'll see Molly when she

too much like the culprit in the dock. She began to hunt

It was a totally unjust charge. However, Mrs. Meigs

She had never expected anything like this from Rich-

pastimes, you didn't have to give up your life to it in

bottom.

A NICE LONG EVENING
No, he wouldn't. That was evident. He was cutting

Mrs. Meigs would side with them, if it came to taking

"That's talking," said Richard. "You've grown used to
having a young person in the house with you. I wouldn't
wish to insinuate that you're not growing any younger
yourself; but it's been a good arrangement, and you'd
be silly to let it lapse."
"I've loved having Molly here," said Mrs. Meigs. "But
I realize I've no claim on her."
"I thought you were a friend of mine," said Richard.
He smiled a little, teasing her. How many things he'd
got past her, ever since he learned to talk, just by assum-
ing that she was "a friend of his!"
He was dressed for a summer evening, in flannel
trousers and bright sweater. He looked very young, with
his rumpled hair and his bright teasing smile. He'd come
to her for help. Was she going to fail him now?
Mrs. Meigs looked from him to Molly.
Molly's eyes were fixed on her, and Molly said slowly,190
A NICE LONG EVENING
"I've done nothing for her but read to her. It saves her
eyes a little."
Mrs. Meigs drew in her breath sharply, and her head
spun. "You thought-my eyes needed saving?" she said
just above her breath.
Molly looked at Richard and was silent. Molly wouldn't
give Mrs. Meigs away. But Molly knew. And if she knew,
she was waiting. Waiting for Mrs. Meigs to make her
confession, and demand that Molly stay and be eyes for
her.
The decision rested with Mrs. Meigs. For the fraction
of a minute she hesitated. Then she said clearly, "I've
taken Molly too much for granted, I think. So have we
all. It will do us all good to miss her."
Richard's smile turned to bitterness. He said sharply,
"You're thinking what a good time you'll have, with
that baby all to yourself."
It was an unjust charge, but she didn't dare refute it.
Molly's hands were twisting in her lap, but Molly's eyes
said for her, "You're against me too. You could stop me
with a word, but you're letting me go."
That, too, was unjust. She might have spoken the word
that would give them to each other, though it seemed
to her the wrong word, and one that they would both
live to regret. Hadn't she handed Cecile over to Tip, and
dared the consequences? Hadn't she handed Natalie back
to Carter, and trusted to luck?
But they called on her to admit the beautiful bright
world was leaving her, little by little. They wanted her
to ask for a guiding hand in the darkness that encroached.
They waited for her announcement that she was now a
burden to others she whose independence had always
been the breath of her nostrils!
Oh, they would be kind to her, of course! Everybody
would be kind, when they found out. But she shrank fromA NICE LONG EVENING
191
that kindness as she would never have shrunk from
harshness.
In the end she would have to accept it. She would
actually have to live on it, in order to go on living at all.
But the end was not yet. She rose slowly, and held herself
carefully erect. An old woman. A woman on whom the
darkness was closing in. But not a beaten woman-yet.
"I'm sorry, Dick," she said. "I'm sorry, Molly."
It wasn't the word they had been waiting for. It was
the best she could do for them. They would remember
their love-they would remember this evening, with the
fresh smell of the Lake in their nostrils, and Molly's un-
done mending lying beside her, and Richard fumbling
for a cigarette and clattering the lid of the box-would
remember it if they lived to be as old as Mrs. Meigs was
now.
But between then and now, a long road stretched ahead
of them. It might be a stony or a dusty road, but at least
it lay ahead. She who had always been so sympathetic
with youth, was bitter against it now. For not only did
the long road lie behind her; here at the end of it, dark-
ness was closing in.XVII
THE next day Molly had Weeks in, to move the guest-
room furniture downstairs, and put Sonny's bed and
chiffonier into what had been the guest-room, next to
Mrs. Meigs's. He couldn't be allowed to sleep alone on the
ground floor. But the furniture could have been moved
after Molly left. Only Molly wanted to keep busy, franti-
cally busy about details, so busy that she wouldn't have
time to think.
It was long past midnight that night when Mrs. Meigs,
lying awake in the dark, heard Molly come upstairs and go
to the baby's room. She stayed in there a long time, lean-
ing over him as he slept. But the next morning, when
it came time for her to leave, she scarcely glanced at
him.
Tip took Molly to the eleven o'clock train. She didn't
want Mrs. Meigs to go to the station with her. They said
good-by casually on the front porch.
But after she was safely stowed in Tip's car, and Tip
had got his balky engine going, Molly jumped out of the
car and came back, to throw her arms around Mrs. Meigs
and kiss her. Mrs. Meigs knew she had got very fond of
Molly, but it made her choke to realize how fond Molly
was of her.
After the bustle of Molly's departure, the house seemed
strangely quiet. The baby was satisfied with Mrs. Meigs
and Anna. But every time Mrs. Meigs looked at him she
thought, "Molly's missing something. He's at the age now
where he develops a new trick every day; and Molly's
192A NICE LONG EVENING
193
missing them all. Whatever happens to her in the future,
these days can never be made up to her."
Well, it didn't do Molly any good for Mrs. Meigs to

dinner on Saturday. Both these invitations were eagerly

Mrs. Bishop was having a nap when Dr. Manning ar-

"If I get the three of them down here at once," said
Mrs. Meigs, "they'll think we have to play Contract.
Older friendships than ours have been broken up over
Contract, I'm afraid. Anyhow I'm not taking any
chances."
"Eyes not any worse, are they?" asked Dr. Manning.
"Certainly not!" snapped Mrs. Meigs. "And I'll thank
you not to talk about my eyes in front of anybody. My
eyes are my own business."
"Haven't you told your family yet that you have
cata-?" began Dr. Manning.
"No, I haven't. There's no reason on earth why I

Dr. Manning began to talk about the garden. When
Mrs. Bishop joined them, he continued to talk about the
garden. He was as loquacious as ever; but the line that
he had kept up for so many years was now missing from
his conversation. His gallant remarks were no longer
forthcoming.
Mrs. Meigs had always been annoyed by his gallantry.
But now that it had ceased, she was chagrined. She'd got
to a pretty pass indeed, if even Dr. Manning no longer
admired her.
She suggested a movie that evening; but it turned out194
A NICE LONG EVENING
that Mrs. Bishop had "promised Grace" she wouldn't go

Meigs had asked them together in order to further their

But Sunday morning Mrs. Bishop wouldn't walk down
to the beach with Mrs. Meigs; and she made an excuse for

Mrs. Meigs had always held it against Grace Whitney
that she pampered her mother. Now she began to suspect,
the pampering was partly Mrs. Bishop's fault. She hinted

Anna grinned, and commented, "You and me aren't
used to really old people around the house, Mis' Meigs."
That was true in a sense, though Mrs. Bishop was
actually twelve years younger than Mrs. Meigs. In other
people's eyes, Mrs. Meigs's image still shone bright. Feel-

sunshine. He was beginning to talk now; this was her
chance to teach him to call her "Grandma."
A week's teaching produced perfect results. That is,
he said "Ga-ga" for "Grandma" and "Ga-ga" for
"cracker." But then, as Mrs. Meigs pointed out, he knew
which he meant each time.
She wrote the news to Molly in Chicago. Molly an-
swered in a long letter. She was staying at a "Girls'
Club," where she had a nice room with a quiet girl from
South Dakota, and they had the use of a laundry in the

think they would be. Everybody at the office was fine to

studying shorthand two evenings a week.A NICE LONG EVENING
195
She didn't say a word about coming up for a week-end.
A direct invitation produced the answer, she was so busy

"She doesn't trust herself too far," thought Mrs. Meigs.
"Or maybe she's made herself some silly promise, that
she'll stick it out for a month, or two months, or three,

Mrs. Meigs's angular backhand sprawled worse than
ever these days. Still she hadn't yet reached the point
where she wrote one line over another. She must keep
Molly in touch. There was always something to report,

For instance, there was Edith's gift of a radio. Edith
was redecorating, as usual. Her old radio didn't go with
her new furniture, so she sent it down for her mother's

If Mrs. Meigs had been consulted in advance, she
wouldn't have given the radio house room. A radio was
the thing devoted children always did send to Mother
when her eyesight failed. But Edith's gift was made in all
innocence. Indeed with her buying tendencies, the odd
thing was that she had owned only two radios; you'd ex-
pect the number to be five or six, at least.
Anna welcomed the radio. Whenever she came into the
front of the house, she turned it on. She liked to do her
dusting to a blare of jazz music.
But it seemed to Mrs. Meigs that her privacy was in-
vaded. Her rooms were filled with the voices of perfect
strangers, all complaining about their lack of success in
their love affairs. Or else they were howling because they
wanted to go back South. Mrs. Meigs was driven to assert
aloud and emphatically, that she hoped she wasn't hinder-
ing them.
There was one song which especially nagged her.196
A NICE LONG EVENING
"You an' the moon," [it ran],
"With you in my arms,
You an' the moon,
Night never seems too long,
With you an' the moon, an' the moon, the moo-oo-oon."

She would have given anything for a flash of his old

He had taken to spending most of his evenings in the

Richard himself didn't make the same estimate; he said

He, who used to see so clearly the folly of making a re-A NICE LONG EVENING

ligion out of what was only a man's bread and butter!
Mrs. Meigs felt as if he were doing it to spite her. That

How long she would have remained in that state there

For forty years Millicent had been giving her mother
advice, and Mrs. Meigs hadn't yet taken any of it. But
Millicent kept right on, in the true crusader spirit. Joan of
Arc must have been an awfully uncomfortable person

Still a good crusader can always show you clearly what
you don't want to do. Millicent might not be congenial,

Molly answered at once, "Do come. I shan't have much
time to entertain you. But you can't guess how I'd like to

"That girl" indeed!
Mrs. Meigs was committed, and didn't intend to draw

A NICE LONG EVENING
down to Chicago alone. She thought first of asking Cecile

It would have to be a friend, then, rather than a relative.

It is queer how quickly a whim can crystallize into a
fact. Four days to a minute after Millicent's call,

And she talked, talked incessantly. About the things

Mrs. Meigs had lived for eighty years without ever dis-

didn't bother. But that innocent remark of Miss Lamb's

Miss Lamb hadn't had much in her life. Mrs. Meigs had

rich and interesting. But if you had had everything, you

Miss Lamb. Already she had an eye out of the restaurant

MONDAY morning they got on the train for home.

The party Tuesday evening was augmented by the

Cecile couldn't stand it to have any one insinuate that

She wouldn't, even when Tip gave her the hint. Very

211
Sipping thirstily, Miss Lamb whispered to Mrs. Meigs,

"Nothing like that. One of six or seven, possibly. I'd

A NICE LONG EVENING
looked to her like on the table. She had felt so well to-

ALONG about the first of September, Weeks shifted
from reminiscence to prophecy. "We'd ought to have two
months of nice weather now," he announced. "Yes, mum.
Fall's the nicest weather of the year, I think. Makes you
feel like working."
Mrs. Meigs could remember many golden "falls" in
Wisconsin. This turned out not to be one of them. By the
middle of September, Weeks was strongly reminded of
the rainy season in the Philippines. But these were cold
storms, coming from the East. Down here on the lake
shore, Mrs. Meigs's household got the full benefit of them.
Mrs. Meigs used never to mind what the weather was.
On a rainy day, indoors was all the pleasanter. Now she
couldn't fill the indoor days with enough pleasant occupa-
tion. The sight of her drenched garden troubled her.
Sonny wasn't getting enough outdoors; and people didn't
get down here to see her the way they would have if the
weather were fair.
Luckily there was one fine day, when Miss Lamb en-
tertained her old friends in her new home. She and
Dr. Manning had actually got married in Chicago, and
had gone for a fortnight's honeymoon, which included
Niagara Falls. On their return they had taken two rooms
in an old-fashioned hotel down town.
"We get our own breakfasts," she reported over the
phone to Mrs. Meigs. "And we go to a movie almost every
evening. I'd no idea being married was as nice as it is."
There wasn't much bridge played the afternoon she
had her old friends in. They had too much to talk about.

She loved his teasing, and she loved that ride on a rainy
afternoon, and being alone with Jack to talk things over.
But she wasn't taking on any good jobs these days. Only a
year ago, she'd fixed things very nicely for Jack and

housekeeping, though both of them were secretly long-

the thing for them, and now it was coming unfixed on the
other side.
That was what you could expect in this world; and the
time had been when Mrs. Meigs would have chuckled, and
waited only for an opportunity to do something about it.
It was queer what a difference a single year could make,
even when it was added to so many.
She nerved herself the next week, and asked her family
down for dinner. It was the first time she'd had them all
together at the bungalow; and it was one way to fill an
evening. For it was the evenings that dragged.
Daytimes she managed to keep fairly busy. The baby
was a great resource. Anna usually bathed and dressed218
A NICE LONG EVENING
him; if Mrs. Meigs tried to do that, she was slow and

But coaxing his meals into him was job enough for two

Two or three times a week, Mrs. Meigs wrote Molly a
bulletin of Sonny's progress. On her long and lonely
Sundays, Molly answered those letters. She commented
eagerly on every detail of the news about Sonny.
But of herself she told very little. They were kind to
her in the office, but they were very busy there, now that
people were returning from their summer vacations.
Molly was getting along all right with her shorthand and
was learning to typewrite. Twice she enclosed a five-

the money aside to keep for him. She hated to spend
money that must have cost so much in self-denial.
Sometimes Mrs. Meigs handed Molly's letters over to
Richard to read. He read them hungrily, but he handed
them back without comment.
Once Mrs. Meigs ventured, "Perhaps you know,
though, what's in this letter."
"She doesn't write to me," said Richard.

219
hell. Everybody admits that. But in time you get used

She had adored him ever since he was born. Not that
she didn't see his faults; but the faults of a grandson are
superior to the virtues of people in whom you take a less
passionate interest. Richard had always felt that he had a
special understanding with her an understanding that
held, if need be, against the generation between them.
"We're in cahoots against the grown people," was his way
of putting it.
She had an uneasy sense that she was failing him now.
She should have thought of ways to distract him from his
brooding. She should have cheered him up, during these
first hard months.
Yet seeing him so quiet, so grave-tracing in him for
the first time a resemblance to his worried father-
Mrs. Meigs no longer felt quite so close to Richard. She
was increasingly conscious of the many, many years that
separated them; she had been almost sixty when he was

He had made up his mind to spend this evening being

mope alone than spoil other people's good times.
Yet though she scarcely heard a word that he read, she

223
ostrich trick. At best, it would gain her a little time-but

Richard, chancing on a choice point in that impassioned

Mrs. Meigs was ashamed of her own suspicions. Yet she
half believed them. She had helped Richard a little to-
night, though. Or wasn't it helping-was it only a piece of
stupidity to expect him to go back to "darling" such a
one and "darling" something else, now that he knew what
the real thing was?
He had called those girls up simply to humor the old
lady. Still he had sounded like himself when he got to talk-
ing to them. It was very little Mrs. Meigs could do for him
now. But she wanted passionately to do that little. The
time might come when she couldn't do anything for any-
body. When that time came, there would be no point nor
purpose in her going on.
Here she sat to-night, sheltered, cared for, amused. It
seemed to her that the hour lay just ahead when people
would be doing things for her, and she could do nothing
in return.
Her heart warmed a little as she thought of the baby,
lying asleep upstairs. Then the last dread laid its cold hand
on her. How much longer would she be able to keep on
doing, even for the baby?XX

Molly would be busy this next week-end. But perhaps

"Is that a promise?" asked Mrs. Meigs.

Molly didn't trust herself to come near her baby; her

Natalie came down to see her the next morning. Natalie

more slipping away from her, with nothing done about it,

Another year she wouldn't be able to see the roses blos-

All these things were right and reasonable, in their way.

warded off every one of these disasters, too, if she'd been

A NICE LONG EVENING
used to, and she'd have showed everybody! Only two days

For two days of freedom now, Mrs. Meigs would have

Perhaps if she went to see the oculist, he could help her

She was going to find out now. Yes, and when she'd

A NICE LONG EVENING

At the door of Dr. Stuart's office building, a beggar

of you are going to clear out before midnight, I'll stay and
read to her."
He and Cecile thought they owned Mrs. Meigs. They
always had thought so, from the time that they were
babies. But Cecile sent him away now, with a message
to Tip.
"You can come and get Caroline to-morrow afternoon,
if you will," she said as he was leaving. "I'll pack that
book in her bag; you can read it to her in the hospital."
He left unwillingly. None of the rest of them wanted
to go, either; and tired as she was, Mrs. Meigs didn't want
them to leave. They were her children, visiting her under
her own roof; and she didn't know where or when she'd
see them next.
When they were all gone, it was after midnight. Cecile
was to sleep in the guest-room downstairs; but she went
up to Mrs. Meigs's room with her, and perched on the
edge of the bed while Mrs. Meigs undressed.
"This is just like old times again, isn't it?" she asked.
"I used to be always coming in to spend the night with
you. It's nice to be getting acquainted with you again."
But she couldn't make herself look at her grandmother
as she said it.
Mrs. Meigs lay stretched out in her own bed. Cecile putA NICE LONG EVENING
241
up the windows and pinned back the curtains. "Shall I

can tip the shade a little, and you'll hardly know it's

"No, put it out," said Mrs. Meigs. "I've never been able
to sleep in a room with the light burning." She had al-
ways demanded a lot of herself. Go through all the regular
motions, and pretend you're not upset. Don't make a
fuss; if you don't make a fuss, it's better for other people.
Cecile put out the little lamp near the door, and came

spoke, it was only to say, "Don't let me forget to pack that
silly book of Dick's. If I do forget, I'll hear from him

She opened the door into the lighted hall and hesitated

and somebody ought to let her know."
"Not to-morrow," said Mrs. Meigs. "The day after,
perhaps, when there's really something to tell her."
Cecile went out, closing the door softly. The house
was very quiet. It was quiet outdoors, too; this late all
the neighbors would be in bed, and now that fall had
come, young lovers no longer strayed down by the lake
shore.
Mrs. Meigs could hear the water lapping on the beach
at the foot of the bluff. Lapping softly, softly; but the
sound seemed to grow louder as she listened. Louder,
louder! She tried not to listen.
She tossed and turned. She heard the old clock on her
tall wardrobe strike, one and then one, and again one.
Did that mean it was getting on for two o'clock? At242
A NICE LONG EVENING
eight the household would be stirring. Couldn't she drop
off now, and know nothing more until it was morning?
It was after two when she finally gave in. Going
through the usual motions wasn't enough to-night. She
could not be quiet any longer, and pretend to rest when
she wasn't resting.
She turned on her bedside lamp and drew on her
dressing-gown. She slipped out into the hall, and across
it to the baby's room. He was sound asleep, in the utter
relaxation of childhood. Mrs. Meigs drew up his blanket,
and patted his fuzzy head. So many nights she had come
in here while he was sleeping; so many, many nights. So
she had covered Jack when he was little, and then Rich-
ard. Some day Sonny too would be a man, and never
think how many times she had stolen to his bedside in
the darkness, and made sure that he was safe and warm,
and listened to his quiet breathing.
When she finally left him, she hesitated in the hall.
She had gone to see if the baby was covered. Now she
should go back to her own room. Go back now and try
to rest. It would be morning all too soon.
In the morning there would be all the bustle of prepara-
tion and departure. Everybody would be watching her;
and before their eyes she'd play her part, as she always had
played it. No fear of her falling down on what other peo-
ple expected of her.
But to-night no one was watching her. She paused a
moment to be sure no one had been disturbed by the
sound of her door. Then she went slowly down the stairs.
From room to room downstairs she passed, turning on
the lights, looking slowly all about her, then turning off
the lights, and passing on to the next room. Her own old
things were all here, brought down here to make the
home where for a little while she had been so happy. The
walnut dining-room set which her father had bought forA NICE LONG EVENING
243
her when she was married; the library table that had stood
in the old house on Cass Street; a tall vase that had be-
longed to her mother, full to-night of late chrysanthe-
mums.
She lived with these things every day. They were so
familiar to her that generally she scarcely noticed them.
But now they brought back all the past with which they
had been associated. Slowly Mrs. Meigs made the circuit
of each room, and stood quiet a minute afterward, and
impressed it all on her memory, just as it was to-night.
She had told no more than the truth when she let Dr.
Stuart understand, she didn't shrink from the risk she
was taking. But she wanted to be able to remember her
home, just as it was to-night.XXII
MRS. MEIGS was amused by this business of going to a
hospital. Our age of specialization had specialized even
being sick. Instead of just staying in bed some morning
and calling the doctor, you packed your bags, and went
off in a car, and were booked in a good deal as if you'd
been going to jail.
Jack had been to the hospital in the morning to secure
a good room for his mother. Then he'd had the happy
thought it would take the institutional look off the room
if he had some flowers waiting for her when she arrived.
The same happy thought had occurred to Natalie, to
Edith, and to Richard. None of them had mentioned it
to the others; they had all gone and ordered flowers, and
all their flowers had been delivered. When Mrs. Meigs
arrived in the middle of the afternoon, every inch of free
space sported a bouquet; and as chrysanthemums were in
season, and the steam was on, the whole place had a fine
weedy smell.
Mrs. Meigs looked around at the floral display and
chuckled, "Well, I didn't know I was as sick as all that."
Richard laughed, but Cecile said, "A bright idea isn't
quite so bright when everybody has it. Hadn't we better
carry some of these into one of the wards?"
"Take mine if you like. But you'd better leave the rest
until bedtime," said Richard. "The whole gang will be
over here to-night, and you can't tell them you've thrown
out their dandelions."
"You're coming too, aren't you?" asked Mrs. Meigs.
244A NICE LONG EVENING

"Coming? I'll be brought," said Richard. "It will be

"Tip and I will be there to-night and until further

Richard's prediction was right. Her son and daughters

authority; but she got up when the first of her guests

The room seemed very full, but she enjoyed the crowd

worst of times she wouldn't have changed them for any-

In her softened mood, indeed, she did exactly what she
had told Jack she wouldn't do: got Estelle aside, and told

too much. But Mr. Jack needn't come around next sum-

all that; but it was time he learned to do his own nagging.
Her party were allowed to stay after visiting hours.
Mrs. Meigs wished that they could have stayed all night.
She was restless in a strange bed; she kept waking and not
knowing where she was.
Just when she finally got settled for a comfortable
sleep, they came disturbing her, and telling her it was
morning. They had operations, it appeared, while civilized
people were still placidly asleep. They hanged people at
the same ungodly hour, she remembered. She wondered246
A NICE LONG EVENING
if all the condemned felt as cross as she did at being dis-

They rode her down to the operating room. She told
Dr. Stuart that it wasn't her feet they were going to
operate on. Dr. Stuart grinned at her protest. "I've been
wishing for years that I had you under my thumb," he
said cheerfully. "Now I've got you there, and I'm going
to make you feel the full weight of my authority."
Mrs. Meigs had dreaded the anaesthetic. She had never
been anaesthetized, and she didn't like the idea of losing
herself. But it turned out that the operation would be done
with a local anaesthetic. Most eye operations were.
It would be done, too, with Jack in the room. "The law
requires some member of your family to be in the room,"
Dr. Stuart explained.
He had selected Jack without asking her; and Jack was
the worst possible choice. Mrs. Meigs would have pre-
ferred Richard; but she realized that in this case she
couldn't very well pass over her children.
She did hold up proceedings, though, while she argued
that Edith should be substituted for Jack. Then Jack
wouldn't accept the substitution; and the operation finally
began with both him and Edith in the room. Dr. Phelps
fumed audibly over the delay. He'd like it better, maybe,
if all his patients were old maids.
She was conscious of a whole circle of people about her
during the operation: Dr. Phelps, Dr. Stuart, an interne,
and three nurses. Dr. Phelps grunted when he wanted
anything; the nurses seemed to understand his grunts.
Once he made some remark aside to Dr. Stuart. But there
was very little speech in the room during the operation.
Mrs. Meigs could hear the click of an instrument
against a dish, the starched rustle of their operating gowns,
footsteps in the corridor outside of the closed door. Her
whole face was numb from the anaesthetic; but everyA NICE LONG EVENING
247
slightest sound seemed to be magnified. Was it possible

the blind?
The operation seemed to go on a very long time: so long

Then quite suddenly it was all over. She was wheeled back

firm capable hand on her wrist.
"You've stood it fine," he said. "Now all you have to
do is lie here and rest."
"How-?" began Mrs. Meigs, and couldn't go on. Her
tongue felt thick and queer; she wanted a drink, but she
couldn't rouse herself to ask for it.
"How long will it be before we take off the bandage
and let you look at people? Four days," said Dr. Stuart
cheerfully. "Only four days. That isn't long to wait, is

That wasn't what she wanted to ask him. She had
wanted to ask, how was Jack? The sight of blood had
always made poor Jack sick. Yet she wasn't sure that there
had been any blood shed in this operation. They peeled
off the cataract, Dr. Stuart had explained to her, "like
peeling an onion." She didn't feel like asking any ques-
tions now. Even if it had made Jack sick, it was over.
She was glad to be back in bed, even in a strange bed.
But it was hard for her to lie still. Drinking through a
tube amused her at first; but she hated being fed. She
wasn't hungry anyhow; and there was no chance of being
interested in eating, when you couldn't see what you ate.
She hated being fussed over, hated the feeling that there
was always somebody in the room with her. Dr. Stuart

249
Living or dying made very little difference. The only

who held that it tasted exactly like glue. Still, it was kind

A NICE LONG EVENING
on this day when everything might have been lost.

ON Sunday she had reached a peak of happiness where
she had nothing in the world left to wish for; and she
looked on Dr. Stuart as her savior from worse than death.
On Monday she and Dr. Stuart had a row which brought
the nurse in from the corridor to see what the trouble
was.
The trouble was that he expected her to wear glasses
now: spectacles, and to wear them all the time. And he
hadn't even bothered to break the news to her. He'd
simply taken it for granted that she wouldn't mind.
But she did mind; she minded very much. Spectacles
ranked in her mind with common-sense shoes and refus-
ing coffee at night for fear it might keep you awake.
"Next thing you'll be telling me to be careful of my
digestion!" she stormed. "Crackers and milk are nice
party refreshments, you'll tell me. And avoid drafts, and
don't go out on rainy days."
The nurse interrupted here. "They've had to take a
lense out of your eye, you know. It must be supplied
somehow."
Dr. Stuart waved the nurse aside. "I've explained all
that. We just won't let her go home until she promises to
behave."
"You won't let me go home?" Mrs. Meigs chuckled.
"How are you going to stop me? I've money enough for a
taxi, and there's a fire escape on the building. Come
around this time to-morrow, and you'll find the bird has
flown."

Her temporary meekness had deserted her; it had been

She had so many things to see to, as soon as they'd let

"I think I will learn," she confided to Cecile. "Contract
gives so many more chances for wild playing. I believe I

"You'll get the bug, and be just as bad as all the rest

"I could join a card club that met in the morning. I've

It occurred to her that there was no point in having
both the bedrooms on her first floor set aside as guest-

and look up her cribbage board, and call the place a "game
room," the way they did in the women's magazines. Or

would be advantages in having a library, now that Rich-
ard had taken to smoking a pipe.
She planned a thousand and one changes in the bunga-A NICE LONG EVENING

low; planned her entertaining, too, and even spoke of it

For the mere return to active life absorbed and thrilled,
and at times almost stunned her. She turned on the radio,
and it blatted "You an' the Moon"; and she thought how
wonderful it was that an old lady, sitting alone in her
bungalow, should know what all the latest love songs

afresh at the gilded and insane world that it opened to
her. She listened for a solid half-hour to Weeks's account
of a summer he had spent touring Nebraska with a car-

on repetition.)
Even her new glasses bothered her less than she had ex-
pected them to. There was one great advantage in wearing
your glasses all the time; you always knew where they
were.
Every morning now she woke keen to see what the day
would bring forth. Every night she lay in bed and
thought of all the things she wanted to do. If she lived to
be a hundred, she hadn't any idea of sitting down and be-
ing bored. Yet for the present she didn't need anything
special to interest her. She wasn't in the least thankful for
the misfortune that had made so many months hideous to
her; only idiots were thankful for misfortune. But like
the dash of lemon that gave a tang to pleasant but insipid
dishes, the sharp taste of disaster so lately averted made
the commonplace round zestful to her now.
She was awakened to her own state by Anna's asking

"Thanksgiving?" echoed Mrs. Meigs in startled tones,258
A NICE LONG EVENING
as if Anna had said "The Day of Judgment.” She caught

vember. But we won't begin to plan for Thanksgiving

Nevertheless she had been jerked to a consciousness of

These days she had the authority of one restored from

Her office gave Molly part of Saturday off. She arrived

Richard, with his back to the room, was fiddling with

Mrs. Meigs was so provoked that she could have

They both looked much older than they had a year ago.
Molly was handsome and a little hard, with her severe

wasn't either business or middle age that ailed them really.
It was never having found a true fit in life.
Richard and Molly didn't need to settle anything to-

An only child who was brought up by a self-sacrificing

rosebuds out of my own bouquet, and twisted them up

"I was thinking of paying a call in the neighborhood,"
said Mrs. Meigs. "But it's such a fine day-"
"Too fine to go indoors," said Mr. Cunningham, clutch-
ing her elbow at the next crossing.
He managed Mrs. Meigs and three books at the
same time. They were walking directly away from
Mrs. Bishop's. Mrs. Meigs remembered a day more than
seventy years ago: a day when the sun shone just like this,
and she'd had to go to school, but when she got there the
teacher was sick, and the children had been given the day
off. She felt just like the little girl who hadn't had to go
to school.A NICE LONG EVENING
275
"Oh, yes!" she heard herself say. "I still keep house.
I enjoy having a home of my own. I like to be by myself
sometimes. Every human being likes to be by himself
sometimes."
She looked like the pattern of meekness: a little old
lady in a widow's bonnet. A little old lady in spectacles,
too. But perhaps it was the spectacles that had done it.
For this was the first time in her life that Mrs. Meigs had
ever scraped acquaintance with a stranger. It was a most
respectable scraping, with references given and names
exchanged; but she knew what Richard would have called
it.
A pick-up: that was what it was. Millicent would have
said, "Mother, at your age!" And of course in a way
Mrs. Meigs agreed with Millicent. At her age, a pick-up
was a lot more fun.
